Cartesian Differential Comonads and New Models of Cartesian Differential Categories
Sacha Ikonicoff, Jean-Simon Pacaud Lemay

Abstract
Cartesian differential categories come equipped with a differential combinator that formalizes the derivative from multi-variable differential calculus, and also provide the categorical semantics of the differential λ \lambda -calculus. An important source of examples of Cartesian differential categories are the coKleisli categories of the comonads of differential categories, where the latter concept provides the categorical semantics of differential linear logic. In this paper, we generalize this construction by introducing Cartesian differential comonads, which are precisely the comonads whose coKleisli categories are Cartesian differential categories, and thus allows for a wider variety of examples of Cartesian differential categories. As such, we construct new examples of Cartesian differential categories from Cartesian differential comonads based on power series, divided power algebras, and Zinbiel algebras.



Acknowledgements.
The authors would like to thank Kristine Bauer and Robin Cockett for useful discussions. The authors would also like to thank Emily Riehl and the anonymous reviewers for editorial comments and suggestions. For this research, the first author was financially supported by a PIMS–CNRS Postdoctoral Fellowship, and the second author was financially supported by a NSERC Postdoctoral Fellowship - Award #: 456414649.

1
Introduction

Cartesian differential categories, introduced by Blute, Cockett, and Seely in
[
5
]
, formalize the theory of multivariable differential calculus by axiomatizing the (total) derivative, and also provide the categorical semantics of the differential
\lambda
-calculus, as introduced by Ehrhard and Regnier in
[
20
]
. Briefly, a Cartesian differential category (Definition
2.3
) is a category with finite products such that each homset is a commutative monoid, which allows for zero maps and sums of maps (Definition
2.1
), and equipped with a differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
, which for every map
{f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
produces its derivatives
\mathsf{D}[f]:A\times A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
. The differential combinator satisfies seven axioms, known as
[CD.1]
to
[CD.7]
, which formalize the basic identities of the (total) derivative from multi-variable differential calculus such as the chain rule, linearity in the vector argument, symmetry of the partial derivatives, etc. Two main examples of Cartesian differential categories are the category of Euclidean spaces and real smooth functions between them (Example
2.8
), and the Lawvere Theory of polynomials over a commutative (semi)ring (Example
2.7
). An important class of examples of Cartesian differential categories, especially for this paper, are the coKleilsi categories of the comonads of differential categories
[
5
, Propostion 3.2.1]
.

Differential categories, introduced by Blute, Cockett, and Seely in
[
4
]
, provide the algebraic foundations of differentiation and the categorical semantics of differential linear logic
[
19
]
. Briefly, a differential category (Example
3.13
) is a symmetric monoidal category with a comonad
\oc
, with comonad structure maps
\delta_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc\oc(A)
and
{\varepsilon_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A}
, such that for each object
A
,
\oc(A)
is a cocommutative comonoid with comultiplication
{\Delta_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)\otimes\oc(A)}
and counit
e_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}I
, and equipped with a deriving transformation, which is a natural transformation
{\mathsf{d}_{A}:\oc(A)\otimes A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)}
. The deriving transformation satisfies five axioms, this time called
[d.1]
to
[d.5]
, which formalize basic identities of differentiation such as the chain rule and the product rule. In the opposite category of a differential category, called a codifferential category, the deriving transformation is a derivation in the classical algebra sense. Examples of differential categories include the opposite category of the category of vector spaces over a field where
\oc
is induced by the free symmetric algebra
[
4
,
3
]
, as well as the opposite category of the category of real vector spaces where
\oc
is instead induced by free
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-rings
[
17
]
.

In a differential category, a smooth map from
A
to
B
is a map of type
\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
. In other words, the (infinitely) differentiable maps are precisely the coKleisli maps. The interpretation of coKleisli maps as smooth can be made precise when the differential category has finite (bi)products where one uses the deriving transformation to define a differential combinator on the coKleisli category. Briefly, for a coKleisli map
f:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
(which is a map of type
A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in the coKleisli category), its derivative
\mathsf{D}[f]:\oc(A\times A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
(which is a map of type
A\times A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in the coKleisli category) is defined as the following composite (in the base category):

\mathsf{D}[f]:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 343.30563pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 343.30563pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 402.0055pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 402.0055pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

where
\pi_{i}
are the product projection maps. One then uses the five deriving transformations axioms
[d.1]
to
[d.5]
to prove that
\mathsf{D}
satisfies the seven differential combinator axioms
[CD.1]
to
[CD.7]
. Thus, for a differential category with finite (bi)products, its coKleisli category is a Cartesian differential category. For the examples where
\oc
is the free symmetric algebra or given by free
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-rings, the resulting coKleisli category can respectively be interpreted as the category of polynomials or real smooth functions over possibly infinite variables (but that will still only depend on a finite number of them), of which the Lawvere theory of polynomials or category of real smooth functions is a sub-Cartesian differential category.

Taking another look at the construction of the differential combinator for the coKleisli category, if we define the natural transformation
\partial_{A}:\oc(A\times A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
as the following composite:

350.24063pt\raise 5.71251pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.6625pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 379.30563pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern then the differential combinator is simply defined by precomposing a coKleisli map
f:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
with
\partial
:

\mathsf{D}[f]:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 20.4707pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.45836pt\raise 5.71251pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.6625pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 80.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 127.91745pt\raise 5.8pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 163.17056pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 163.17056pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

It is important to stress that this is the composition in the base category and not the composition in the coKleisli category. Thus, the properties of the differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
in the coKleisli category are fully captured by the properties of the natural transformation
\partial
in the base category, which in turn are a result of the axioms of the deriving transformation
\mathsf{d}
. However, observe that the type of
\partial_{A}:\oc(A\times A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
does not involve any monoidal structure. In fact, if one starts with a comonad whose coKleisli category is a Cartesian differential category, it is always possible to construct
\partial
, and to show that
\mathsf{D}[-]=-\circ\partial
, but it is not always possible to extract a monoidal structure on the base category. Thus, if one’s goal is simply to build Cartesian differential categories from coKleisli categories, then a monoidal structure
\otimes
or a deriving transformation
\mathsf{d}
, or even a comonoid structure
\Delta
and
e
, are not always necessary. Therefore, the objective of this paper is to precisely characterize the comonads whose coKleisli categories are Cartesian differential categories. To this end, in this paper we introduce the novel notion of a Cartesian differential comonad.

Cartesian differential comonads are precisely the comonads whose coKleisli categories are Cartesian differential categories. Briefly, a Cartesian differential comonad is a comonad
\oc
on a category with finite biproducts equipped with a differential combinator transformation, which is a natural transformation
\partial_{A}:\oc(A\times A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
which satisfies six axioms called
[dc.1]
to
[dc.6]
(Definition
3.1
). The axioms of a differential combinator transformation are analogues of the axioms of a differential combinator. Thus, the coKleisli category of a Cartesian differential comonad is a Cartesian differential category where the differential combinator is defined by precomposition with the differential combinator transformation (Theorem
3.5
). This is proven by reasonably straightforward calculations, but one must be careful when translating back and forth between the base category and the coKleisli category. Conversely, a comonad on a category with finite biproduct whose coKleisli category is a Cartesian differential category is in fact a Cartesian differential comonad, where the differential combinator transformation is the derivative of the identity map
{1_{\oc(A)}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)}
seen as a coKleisli map
A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
(Proposition
3.6
). Using this, since we already know that the coKleisli category of a differential category is a Cartesian differential category, it immediately follows that the comonad of a differential category is a Cartesian differential comonad, where the differential combinator transformation is precisely the one defined above. Therefore, Cartesian differential comonads and differential combinator transformations are indeed generalizations of differential categories and deriving transformations. However, Cartesian differential comonads are a strict generalization since, as mentioned, they can be defined without the need of a monoidal structure. A very simple separating example is the identity comonad on any category with finite biproducts, where the differential combinator transformation is simply the second projection map (Example
3.16
). While this example is trivial, it recaptures the fact that any category with finite biproducts is a Cartesian differential category and this example clearly works without any extra monoidal structure, and thus is not a differential category example. Therefore, Cartesian differential comonads allow for a wider variety of examples of Cartesian differential categories. As such, in this paper we present three new interesting examples of Cartesian differential comonads, which are not differential categories, and their induced Cartesian differential categories. These three examples are respectively based on formal power series, divided power algebras, and Zinbiel algebras. It is worth mentioning that these new examples arise more naturally as coCartesian differential monads (Example
3.14
), the dual notion of Cartesian differential comonads, and thus it is the opposite of the Kleisli category which is a Cartesian differential category.

The first example (Section
5
) is based on reduced power series. Recall that a formal power series is said to be reduced if it has no constant/degree 0 term. While the composition of arbitrary multivariable formal power series is not always well defined, due to their constant terms, the composition of reduced multivariable power series is always well-defined
[
7
, Section 4.1]
, and so we may construct categories of reduced power series. Also, it is well known that power series are always and easily differentiable, similarly to polynomials, and that the derivative of a reduced multivariable power series is again reduced. Motivated by capturing power series differentiation, we show that the free reduced power series algebra monad
[
22
, Section 1.4.3]
is a coCartesian differential monad (Corollary
5.4
) whose monad structure is based on reduced power series composition (Lemma
5.1
) and whose differential combinator transformation is induced by standard power series differentiation (Proposition
5.2
). Furthermore, the Lawvere theory of reduced power series (Example
5.5
) is a sub-Cartesian differential category of the opposite category of the resulting Kleisli category.

The second new example (Section
6
) is based on divided power algebras. Divided power algebras, defined by Cartan
[
8
]
, are commutative non-unital associative algebras equipped with additional operations
(-)^{[n]}
for all strictly positive integer
n
, satisfying some relations (Definition
6.1
). In characteristic
0
, divided power algebras correspond precisely to commutative non-unital associative algebras. In positive characteristics, however, the two notions diverge. There exist free divided power algebras and we show that the free divided power algebra monad
[
29
, Section 10, Théorème 1 and 2]
is a coCartesian differential monad (Corollary
6.5
). Free divided power algebras correspond to the algebra of reduced divided power polynomials. Thus the differential combinator transformation of this example (Proposition
6.3
) captures differentiating divided power polynomials
[
26
]
. In particular, the Lawvere theory of reduced divided power polynomials (Example
6.6
) is a sub-Cartesian differential category of the opposite category of the Kleisli category of the free divided power algebra monad.

The third new example (Section
7
), and perhaps the most exotic example in this paper, is based on Zinbiel algebras. The notion of Zinbiel algebra was introduced by Loday
[
28
]
and also further studied by Dokas
[
18
]
. A Zinbiel algebra is a vector space
A
endowed with a non-associative and non-commutative bilinear operation
<
. Using the Zinbiel product, every Zinbiel algebra can be turned into a commutative non-unital associative algebra. The underlying vector space of free Zinbiel algebras is the same as the underlying vector space of the non-unital tensor algebra. Therefore, free Zinbiel algebras are spanned by (non-empty) associative words and equipped with a product
<
(which is sometimes referred to as the semi-shuffle product). The resulting commutative associative algebra is then precisely the non-unital shuffle algebra over
V
. We show that the free Zinbiel algebra monad
[
28
, Proposition 1.8]
is a coCartesian differential monad (Corollary
7.6
) whose differential combinator transformation (Proposition
7.4
) corresponds to differentiating non-commutative polynomials with respect to the Zinbiel product. The resulting Cartesian differential category can be understood as the category of reduced non-commutative polynomials where the composition is defined using the Zinbiel product, which we simply call Zinbiel polynomials. As such, the Lawvere theory of Zinbiel polynomials is a new exotic example of a Cartesian differential category. It is worth mentioning that the shuffle algebra has been previously studied as an example of another generalization of differential categories in
[
1
]
, but not from the point of view of Zinbiel algebras.

An important class of maps in a Cartesian differential category are the
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps (Definition
2.4
), also often simply called linear maps
[
5
]
. A map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if its derivative
\mathsf{D}[f]:A\times A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is equal to
f
evaluated in its second argument, that is,
\mathsf{D}[f]=f\circ\pi_{1}
(where
\pi_{1}
is the projection map of the
second
argument). A
\mathsf{D}
-linear map should be thought of as being of degree 1, and thus does not have any higher-order derivative. Thus, in many examples,
\mathsf{D}
-linearity often coincides with the classical notion of linearity. For example, in the Cartesian differential category of real smooth functions, a smooth function is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if and only if it is
\mathbb{R}
-linear. For a Cartesian differential comonad, every map of the base category provides a
\mathsf{D}
-linear map in the coKleisli category. However, it is not necessarily the case that the base category is isomorphic to the subcategory of
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps of the coKleisli category. Indeed, a simple example of such a case is the trivial Cartesian differential comonad which maps every object to the zero object and thus every coKleisli map is a zero map. Clearly, if the base category is non-trivial it will not be equivalent to the subcategory of
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps. Instead, it is possible to provide necessary and sufficient conditions for the base category to be isomorphic to the subcategory of
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps of the coKleisli category. It turns out that this is precisely the case when the Cartesian differential comonad comes equipped with a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit, which is a natural transformation
{\eta_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)}
satisfying two axioms
[du.1]
and
[du.2]
(Definition
3.8
). If it exists, a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit is unique and it is equivalent to an isomorphism between the base category and the subcategory of
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps of the coKleisli category (Proposition
3.11
). In the context of differential categories, specifically in categorical models of differential linear logic, the
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit is precisely the codereliction
[
4
,
3
,
19
]
. The Cartesian differential comonads based on power series, or divided power algebras, or Zinbiel algebras all come equipped with
\mathsf{D}
-linear units.

In
[
6
]
, Blute, Cockett, and Seely give a characterization of the Cartesian differential categories which are the coKleisli categories of differential categories. Generalizing their approach, it is also possible to precisely characterize the Cartesian differential categories which are the coKleisli categories of Cartesian differential comonads (Section
4
). To this end, we must work with abstract coKleisli categories (Definition
4.1
), which gives a description of coKleisli categories without starting from a comonad. Abstract coKleisli categories are the dual notion of Führmann’s thunk-force-categories
[
24
]
, which instead do the same for Kleisli categories. Every abstract coKleisli category is canonically the coKleisli category of a comonad on a certain subcategory (Lemma
4.4
), and conversely, the coKleisli category of any comonad is an abstract coKleisli category (Lemma
4.12
). In this paper, we introduce Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli categories (Definition
4.9
) which are abstract coKleisli categories that are also Cartesian differential categories such that the differential combinator and the abstract coKleisli structure are compatible. Every Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli category is canonically the coKleisli category of a Cartesian differential comonad over a certain subcategory of
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps (Proposition
4.10
), and conversely, the coKleisli category of a Cartesian differential comonad is a Cartesian differential abstract category (Proposition
4.16
).

In conclusion, Cartesian differential comonads give a minimum general construction to build coKleisli categories which are Cartesian differential categories. The theory of Cartesian differential comonads also highlights the interaction between the coKleisli structure and the differential combinator. While Cartesian differential comonads recapture some of the notions of differential categories, they are more general. Therefore, Cartesian differential comonads open the door to a variety of new, interesting, and exotic examples of Cartesian differential categories. New examples will be particularly important and of interest, especially since applications of Cartesian differential categories keep being developed, especially in the fields of machine learning and automatic differentiation.

Conventions:

In an arbitrary category, we use the classical notation for composition as opposed to diagrammatic order which was used in other papers on differential categories (such as in
[
5
,
27
]
for example). The composite map
{g\circ f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C}
is the map that first does
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
then
g:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
. We denote identity maps as
{1_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A}
.

2
Cartesian Differential Categories

In this background section we quickly review Cartesian differential categories
[
5
]
.

The underlying structure of a Cartesian differential category is that of a Cartesian left additive category, which in particular allows one to have zero maps and sums of maps, while also allowing for maps which do not preserve said sums or zeros. Maps which do preserve the additive structure are called
additive
maps. Categorically speaking, a left additive category is a category which is
skew
-enriched over the category of commutative monoids and monoid morphisms
[
25
]
. Then a Cartesian left additive category is a left additive category with finite products such that the product structure is compatible with the commutative monoid structure, that is, the projection maps are additive. Note that since we are working with commutative monoids, we do not assume that our Cartesian left additive categories necessarily come equipped with additive inverses, or in other words negatives. For a category with (chosen) finite products we denote the (chosen) terminal object as
\top
, the binary product of objects
A
and
B
by
A\times B
with projection maps
\pi_{0}:A\times B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
\pi_{1}:A\times B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and pairing operation
\langle-,-\rangle
, so that for maps
f:C\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
g:C\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
,
\langle f,g\rangle:C\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A\times B
is the unique map such that
\pi_{0}\circ\langle f,g\rangle=f
and
\pi_{1}\circ\langle f,g\rangle=g
. As such, the product of maps
h:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
k:C\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}D
is the map
h\times k:A\times C\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B\times D
defined as
h\times k=\langle h\circ\pi_{0},k\circ\pi_{1}\rangle
.

Definition 2.1

A
left additive category

[
5
, Definition 1.1.1]
is a category
\mathbb{X}
such that each hom-set
\mathbb{X}(A,B)
is a commutative monoid, with binary addition
+:\mathbb{X}(A,B)\times\mathbb{X}(A,B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}(A,B)
,
(f,g)\mapsto f+g
and zero
0\in\mathbb{X}(A,B)
, and such that pre-composition preserves the additive structure, that is, for any maps
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
,
g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, and
x:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, the following equality holds:

\displaystyle(f+g)\circ x=f\circ x+g\circ x

\displaystyle 0\circ x=0

A map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is said to be
additive

[
5
, Definition 1.1.1]
if post-composition by
f
preserves the additive structure, that is, for any maps
x:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
y:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, the following equality holds:

\displaystyle f\circ(x+y)=f\circ x+f\circ y

\displaystyle f\circ 0=0

A
Cartesian left additive category

[
27
, Definition 2.3]
is a left additive category
\mathbb{X}
which has finite products and such that all the projection maps
\pi_{0}:A\times B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
\pi_{1}:A\times B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
are additive.

We note that the definition of a Cartesian left additive category presented here is not precisely that given in
[
5
, Definition 1.2.1]
, but was shown to be equivalent in
[
27
, Lemma 2.4]
. Also note that in a Cartesian left additive category, the unique map to the terminal object
\top
is the zero map
{0:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\top}
. Here are now some important maps for Cartesian differential categories that can be defined in any Cartesian left additive category:

Definition 2.2

In a Cartesian left additive category
\mathbb{X}
:

(i)

For each pair of objects
A
and
B
, define the
injection maps

\iota_{0}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A\times B
and
\iota_{1}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A\times B
respectively as
\iota_{0}:=\langle 1_{A},0\rangle
and
\iota_{1}:=\langle 0,1_{B}\rangle

(ii)

For each object
A
, define the
sum map

\nabla_{A}:A\times A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
as
\nabla_{A}:=\pi_{0}+\pi_{1}
.

(iii)

For each object
A
, define the
lifting map

\ell_{A}:A\times A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A\times A)\times(A\times A)
as follows
\ell:=\iota_{0}\times\iota_{1}
.

(iv)

For each object
A
define the
interchange map

c_{A}:(A\times A)\times(A\times A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A\times A)\times(A\times A)
as follows
c_{A}:=\left\langle\pi_{0}\times\pi_{0},\pi_{1}\times\pi_{1}\right\rangle
.

It is important to note that while
c
is natural in the expected sense, the injection maps
\iota_{j}
, the sum map
\nabla
, and the lifting map
\ell
are not natural transformations. Instead, they are natural only with respect to additive maps. In particular, since the injection maps are not natural map for arbitrary maps, it follows that these injection maps do not make the product a coproduct, and therefore not a biproduct. However, the biproduct identities still hold in a Cartesian left additive category in the sense that the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\pi_{0}\circ\iota_{0}=1_{A}

\displaystyle\pi_{0}\circ\iota_{1}=0

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\circ\iota_{0}=0

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\circ\iota_{1}=1_{B}

\displaystyle\iota_{0}\circ\pi_{0}+\iota_{1}\circ\pi_{1}=1_{A\times B}

With all this said, it turns out that a category with finite biproducts is precisely a Cartesian left additive category where every map is additive
[
25
, Example 2.3.(ii)]
. In that case, note the injection maps of Definition
2.2
.(
i
) are precisely the injection maps of the coproduct, while the sum map of Definition
2.2
.(
ii
) is the co-diagonal map of the coproduct.

Cartesian differential categories are Cartesian left additive categories which come equipped with a differential combinator, which in turn is axiomatized by the basic properties of the directional derivative from multivariable differential calculus. There are various equivalent ways of expressing the axioms of a Cartesian differential category. Here we have chosen the one found in
[
27
, Definition 2.6]
(using the notation for Cartesian left additive categories introduced above). It is important to notice that in the following definition, unlike in the original paper
[
5
]
and other early works on Cartesian differential categories, we use the convention used in the more recent works where the linear argument of
\mathsf{D}[f]
is its second argument rather than its first argument.

Definition 2.3

A
Cartesian differential category

[
5
, Definition 2.1.1]
is a Cartesian left additive category
\mathbb{X}
equipped with a
differential combinator

\mathsf{D}
, which is a family of operators:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}:\mathbb{X}(A,B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}(A\times A,B)

\displaystyle\frac{f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}{\mathsf{D}[f]:A\times A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}

and such that the following seven axioms hold:

[CD.1]

\mathsf{D}[f+g]=\mathsf{D}[f]+\mathsf{D}[g]
and
\mathsf{D}[0]=0

[CD.2]

\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\left(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A}\right)=\mathsf{D}[f]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\mathsf{D}[f]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})
and
\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\iota_{0}=0

[CD.3]

\mathsf{D}[1_{A}]=\pi_{1}
,
\mathsf{D}[\pi_{0}]=\pi_{0}\circ\pi_{1}
, and
\mathsf{D}[\pi_{1}]=\pi_{0}\circ\pi_{1}

[CD.4]

\mathsf{D}[\left\langle f,g\right\rangle]=\left\langle\mathsf{D}[f],\mathsf{D}[g]\right\rangle

[CD.5]

\mathsf{D}[g\circ f]=\mathsf{D}[g]\circ\langle f\circ\pi_{0},\mathsf{D}[f]\rangle

[CD.6]

\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\circ\ell_{A}=\mathsf{D}[f]

[CD.7]

\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\circ c_{A}=\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]

For a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
,
\mathsf{D}[f]:A\times A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is called the derivative of
f
.

A discussion on the intuition for the differential combinator axioms can be found in
[
5
, Remark 2.1.3]
. It is also worth mentioning that there is a sound and complete term logic for Cartesian differential categories
[
5
, Section 4]
.

An important class of maps in a Cartesian differential category is the class of linear maps. In this paper, however, we borrow the terminology from
[
25
]
and will instead call them
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps. This terminology will help distinguish between the classical notion of linearity from commutative algebra and the Cartesian differential category notion of linearity.

Definition 2.4

In a Cartesian differential category
\mathbb{X}
with differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
, a map
f
is said to be
\mathsf{D}
-linear

[
5
, Definition 2.2.1]
if
\mathsf{D}[f]=f\circ\pi_{1}
. Define the subcategory of linear maps
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}]
to be the category whose objects are the same as
\mathbb{X}
and whose maps are
\mathsf{D}
-linear in
\mathbb{X}
, and let
\mathsf{U}:\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
be the obvious forgetful functor.

By
[
5
, Lemma 2.2.2]
, every
\mathsf{D}
-linear is additive, and therefore it follows that
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}]
has finite biproducts, and is thus also a Cartesian left additive category (where every map is additive) such that the forgetful functor
{\mathsf{U}:\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}}
preserves the Cartesian left additive structure strictly. It is important to note that although additive and linear maps often coincide in many examples of Cartesian differential category, in an arbitrary Cartesian differential category, not every additive map is necessarily linear. Here are now some useful properties of linear maps:

Lemma 2.5

[
5
, Lemma 2.2.2, Corollary 2.2.3]
In a Cartesian differential category
\mathbb{X}
with differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
:

(i)

If
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear then
f
is additive;

(ii)

For any map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, define
\mathsf{L}[f]:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
(called the linearization of
f

[
14
, Definition 3.1]
) as the following composite:

\mathsf{L}[f]:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.62375pt\raise 4.88124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.83124pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\times A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 117.65779pt\raise 6.15pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{D}[f]}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 157.6456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 157.6456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Then
\mathsf{L}[f]
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear.

(iii)

A map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if and only if
f=\mathsf{L}[f]
.

(iv)

If
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear then for every map
g:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
,
\mathsf{D}[g\circ f]=\mathsf{D}[g]\circ(f\times f)
;

(v)

If
g:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear then for every map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
,
\mathsf{D}[g\circ f]=g\circ\mathsf{D}[f]
.

We conclude this section with some examples of well-known Cartesian differential categories and their
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps. The first three examples are based on the standard notions of differentiating linear functions, polynomials, and smooth functions respectively.

Example 2.6

Any category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproduct is a Cartesian differential category where the differential combinator is defined by precomposing with the second projection map:
\mathsf{D}[f]=f\circ\pi_{1}
.
In this case, every map is
\mathsf{D}
-linear by definition and so
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}]=\mathbb{X}
. As a particular example, let
\mathbb{F}
be a field and let
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}
be the category of
\mathbb{F}
-vector spaces and
\mathbb{F}
-linear maps between them. Then
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}
is a Cartesian differential category where for an
\mathbb{F}
-linear map
{f:V\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}W}
, its derivative
\mathsf{D}[f]:V\times V\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}W
is defined as
\mathsf{D}[f](v,w)=f(w)
.

Example 2.7

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a field. Define the category
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}
whose object are
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where a map
{P:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
is a
m
-tuple of polynomials in
n
variables, that is,
P=\langle p_{1}(\vec{x}),\ldots,p_{m}(\vec{x})\rangle
with
p_{i}(\vec{x})\in\mathbb{F}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
.
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}
is a Cartesian differential category where the differential combinator is given by the standard differentiation of polynomials, that is, for a map
{P:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
, with
P=\langle p_{1}(\vec{x}),\ldots,p_{m}(\vec{x})\rangle
, its derivative
\mathsf{D}[P]:n\times n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m
is defined as the tuple of the sum of the partial derivatives of the polynomials
p_{i}(\vec{x})
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}[P](\vec{x},\vec{y}):=\left(\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial p_{1}(\vec{x})}{\partial x_{i}}y_{i},\ldots,\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial p_{n}(\vec{x})}{\partial x_{i}}y_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial p_{j}(\vec{x})}{\partial x_{i}}y_{i}\in\mathbb{F}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}]

A map
P:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if it of the form:

\displaystyle P=\left\langle\sum\limits^{n}_{i=0}r_{i,1}x_{i},\ldots,\sum\limits^{n}_{i=0}r_{i,m}x_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle r_{i,j}\in\mathbb{F}

In other words,
P=\langle p_{1}(\vec{x}),\ldots,p_{m}(\vec{x})\rangle
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if and only if each
p_{i}(\vec{x})
induces an
\mathbb{F}
-linear map
{\mathbb{F}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{F}}
. As such,
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}]
is equivalent to the category
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{LIN}
whose objects are the finite powers
\mathbb{F}^{n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
(including the singleton
\mathbb{F}^{0}=\{0\}
) and whose maps are
\mathbb{F}
-linear maps
{\mathbb{F}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{F}^{m}}
. We note that this example can be generalized to the category of polynomials over an arbitrary commutative (semi)ring.

Example 2.8

Let
\mathbb{R}
be the set of real numbers. Define
\mathsf{SMOOTH}
as the category whose objects are the Euclidean real vector spaces
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and whose maps are the real smooth functions
{F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{R}^{m}}
between them.
\mathsf{SMOOTH}
is a Cartesian differential category, arguably the canonical example, where the differential combinator is defined as the directional derivative of a smooth function. So for a smooth function
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{R}^{m}
, its derivative
{\mathsf{D}[F]:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{R}^{m}}
is then defined as:

\mathsf{D}[F](\vec{x},\vec{y}):=\left\langle\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial x_{i}}(\vec{x})y_{i},\ldots,\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x_{i}}(\vec{x})y_{i}\right\rangle

Note that
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}
is a sub-Cartesian differential category of
\mathsf{SMOOTH}
. A smooth function
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{R}^{m}
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if and only if it is
\mathbb{R}
-linear in the classical sense. Therefore,
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathsf{SMOOTH}]=\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{LIN}
.

Example 2.9

An important source of examples of Cartesian differential categories, especially for this paper, are those which arise as the coKleisli category of a differential category
[
4
,
6
]
. We will review this example in Example
3.13
.

There are many other interesting (and sometimes very exotic) examples of Cartesian differential categories in the literature. See
[
25
,
14
]
for lists of more examples of Cartesian differential categories. Interesting generalizations of Cartesian differential categories include
R
-linear Cartesian differential categories
[
25
]
(which adds the ability of scalar multiplication by a commutative ring
R
), generalized Cartesian differential categories
[
16
]
(which generalizes the notion of differential calculus of smooth functions between open subsets), differential restriction categories
[
11
]
(which generalizes the notion of differential calculus of partially defined smooth functions), and tangent categories
[
10
]
(which generalizes the notion of differential calculus over smooth manifolds).

3
Cartesian Differential Comonads

In this section, we introduce the main novel concept of study in this paper: Cartesian differential comonads, which are precisely the comonads whose coKleisli category is a Cartesian differential category. This is a generalization of
[
5
, Proposition 3.2.1]
, which states that the coKleisli category of the comonad of a differential category is a Cartesian differential category. The generalization comes from the fact that a Cartesian differential comonad can be defined without the need for a monoidal product or cocommutative comonoid structure on the comonad’s coalgebras. As such, this allows for a wider variety of examples of Cartesian differential categories. Briefly, a Cartesian differential comonad is a comonad on a category with finite biproducts, which comes equipped with a differential combinator transformation, which generalizes the notion of a deriving transformation in a differential category
[
4
,
3
]
. The induced differential combinator is defined by precomposing a coKleisli map with the differential combinator transformation (with respect to composition in the base category). Conversely, a comonad whose coKleisli category is a Cartesian differential category is a Cartesian differential comonad, where the differential combinator transformation is defined using the coKleisli category’s differential combinator. We point out that this statement, regarding comonads whose coKleisli categories are Cartesian differential categories, is a novel observation and shows us that even if one cannot extract a monoidal product on the base category from the coKleisli category, it is possible to obtain a natural transformation which captures differentiation. Lastly, we will also study the case where the
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps of the coKleisli category correspond to the maps of the base category. The situation arises precisely in the presence of what we call a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit, which generalizes the notion of a codereliction from differential linear logic
[
4
,
3
,
21
,
19
]
.

If only to introduce notation, recall that a comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
is a triple
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
consisting of a functor
{\oc:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}}
, and two natural transformations
\delta_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc\oc(A)
, called the comonad comultiplication, and
{\varepsilon_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A}
, called the comonad counit, and such that the following equalities hold:

\begin{gathered}\delta_{\oc(A)}\circ\delta_{A}=\oc(\delta_{A})\circ\delta_{A}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\delta_{A}=1_{\oc(A)}=\oc(\varepsilon_{A})\circ\delta_{A}\end{gathered}

(1)

Definition 3.1

For a comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts, a
differential combinator transformation
on
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
is a natural transformation
\partial_{A}:\oc(A\times A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
such that the following diagrams commute:

[dc.1]

Zero Rule:

\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\oc(\iota_{0})}
\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)}

where
\iota_{0}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A\times A
is defined as in Definition
2.2
.(
i
).

[dc.2]

Additive Rule:

\textstyle{\oc\!\left(A\times(A\times A)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\oc(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A})}
\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)}

where
\nabla_{A}:A\times A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is defined as in Definition
2.2
.(
ii
).

[dc.3]

Linear Rule:

\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{A\times A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{A}}
\textstyle{A\times A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\textstyle{A}

[dc.4]

Chain Rule:

\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{A\times A}}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\oc\left(\langle\oc(\pi_{0}),\partial_{A}\rangle\right)}
\textstyle{\oc\!\left(\oc(A)\times\oc(A)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{\oc(A)}}
\textstyle{\oc\oc(A)}

[dc.5]

Lift Rule:

\textstyle{\oc\left(A\times A\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\oc(\ell_{A})}
\textstyle{\oc\!\left((A\times A)\times(A\times A)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A\times A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)}

where
\ell_{A}:A\times A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A\times A)\times(A\times A)
is defined as in Definition
2.2
.(
iii
).

[dc.6]

Symmetry Rule:

\textstyle{\oc\left((A\times A)\times(A\times A)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A\times A}}
\scriptstyle{\oc(c_{A})}
\textstyle{\oc\!\left((A\times A)\times(A\times A)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A\times A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)}

where
c_{A}:(A\times A)\times(A\times A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A\times A)\times(A\times A)
is defined as in Definition
2.2
.(
iv
).

A
Cartesian differential comonad
on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts is a quadruple
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
consisting of a comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
and a differential combinator transformation
\partial
on
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
.

As the name suggests, the differential combinator transformations axioms correspond to some of the axioms a differential combinator. The zero rule
[dc.1]
and the additive rule
[dc.2]
correspond to
[CD.2]
, the linear rule
[dc.3]
corresponds to
[CD.3]
, the chain rule
[dc.4]
corresponds to
[CD.5]
, the lift rule corresponds to
[CD.6]
, and lastly the symmetry rule
[dc.6]
corresponds to
[CD.7]
.

Our goal is now to show that the coKleisli category of a Cartesian differential comonad is a Cartesian differential category. As we will be working with coKleisli categories, we will use the notation found in
[
6
]
and use interpretation brackets
\llbracket-\rrbracket
to help distinguish between composition in the base category and coKleisli composition. So for a comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
on a category
\mathbb{X}
, let
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
denote its coKleisli category, which is the category whose objects are the same as
\mathbb{X}
and where a map
A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in the coKleisli category is map of type
\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in the base category, that is,
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}(A,B)=\mathbb{X}(\oc(A),B)
. Composition of coKleisli maps
{\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
and
\llbracket g\rrbracket:\oc(B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket g\circ f\rrbracket:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 11.34993pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 22.62889pt\raise 5.71251pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.6625pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 47.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
