3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 167.83052pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 167.83052pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle\llbracket g\circ f\rrbracket=\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\delta_{A}

The identity maps in the coKleisli category is given by the comonad counit:

\displaystyle\llbracket 1_{A}\rrbracket:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 11.34993pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.56438pt\raise 4.88124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.83124pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 71.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Let
\mathsf{F}_{\oc}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
be the standard inclusion functor which is defined on objects as
\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(A)=A
and on maps
{f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 11.34993pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.56438pt\raise 4.88124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.83124pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 47.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.03285pt\raise 5.8pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 96.28596pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 96.28596pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket=f\circ\varepsilon_{A}

A key map in this story is the coKleisli map whose interpretation is the identity map in the base category. So for every object
A
, define the map
\varphi_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
in the coKleisli category as follows:

\begin{gathered}\llbracket\varphi_{A}\rrbracket:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 11.34993pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.11246pt\raise 6.195pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.865pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1_{\oc(A)}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 71.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(2)

Here are now some useful identities in the coKleisli category:

Lemma 3.2

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
be a comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
. Then the following equalities hold:

(i)

\llbracket g\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket=\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\oc(f)

(ii)

\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(g)\circ f\rrbracket=g\circ\llbracket f\rrbracket

(iii)

\llbracket f\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\varphi_{A}\rrbracket

(iv)

\llbracket\varphi_{B}\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket=\oc(f)

(v)

\llbracket\varphi_{\oc(A)}\circ\varphi_{A}\rrbracket=\delta_{A}

It is a well-known result that if the base category has finite products, then so does the coKleisli category.

Lemma 3.3

[
31
, Dual of Proposition 2.2]
Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
be a comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite products. Then the coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
has finite products where:

(i)

The product
\times
on objects is defined as as in
\mathbb{X}
;

(ii)

The projection maps
\llbracket\pi_{0}\rrbracket:\oc(A\times B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
\llbracket\pi_{1}\rrbracket:\oc(A\times B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
are defined respectively as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket\pi_{0}\rrbracket:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 20.72643pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.72643pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A\times B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.37135pt\raise 5.06874pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.64374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{A\times B}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.72643pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 56.72643pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\times B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 99.75125pt\raise 4.88124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.83124pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 124.41545pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 124.41545pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle\llbracket\pi_{0}\rrbracket=\pi_{0}\circ\varepsilon_{A\times B}

\displaystyle\llbracket\pi_{1}\rrbracket:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 20.72643pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.72643pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A\times B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.37135pt\raise 5.06874pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.64374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{A\times B}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.72643pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 56.72643pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\times B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 99.75125pt\raise 4.88124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.83124pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 124.41545pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 124.41545pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle\llbracket\pi_{1}\rrbracket=\pi_{1}\circ\varepsilon_{A\times B}

(iii)

The pairing of coKleisli maps
\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(C)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
\llbracket g\rrbracket:\oc(C)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is defined as in
\mathbb{X}
, that is:

\displaystyle\llbracket\langle f,g\rangle\rrbracket:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 11.50334pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.50334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.7756pt\raise 8.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\left\langle\llbracket f\rrbracket,\llbracket g\rrbracket\right\rangle}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.50334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 71.50334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\times B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(iv)

The terminal object
\top
is the same as in
\mathbb{X}
.

(v)

For coKleisli maps
\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
and
\llbracket g\rrbracket:\oc(B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}D
, their product is equal to the following composite:

\displaystyle\llbracket f\times g\rrbracket:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 20.72643pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.72643pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A\times B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.1176pt\raise 8.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\langle\oc(\pi_{0}),\oc(\pi_{1})\rangle}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.72643pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 62.72643pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\times\oc(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 115.17928pt\raise 5.83499pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\llbracket f\rrbracket\times\llbracket g\rrbracket}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 155.9431pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 155.9431pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C\times D}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle\llbracket f\times g\rrbracket=\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\times\llbracket g\rrbracket\right)\circ\langle\oc(\pi_{0}),\oc(\pi_{1})\rangle

(vi)

\mathsf{F}_{\oc}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
preserves the finite product strictly, that is, the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(A\times B)

\displaystyle=A\times B

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\top)

\displaystyle=\top

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\pi_{0})\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\pi_{0}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\pi_{1})\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\pi_{1}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\langle f,g\rangle\right)\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\langle\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f),\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(g)\rangle\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(f\times g\right)\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\times\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(g)\rrbracket

If the base category is also Cartesian left additive, then so is the coKleisli category in a canonical way, that is, where the additive structure is simply that of the base category.

Lemma 3.4

[
5
, Proposition 1.3.3]
Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
be a comonad on a Cartesian left additive category
\mathbb{X}
with finite products. Then the coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
is a Cartesian left additive category where the finite product structure is given in Lemma
3.3
and where:

(i)

The sum of coKleisli maps
\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
\llbracket g\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is defined as in
\mathbb{X}
, that is:

\llbracket f+g\rrbracket=\llbracket f\rrbracket+\llbracket g\rrbracket

(ii)

The zero
\llbracket 0\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is the same as in
\mathbb{X}
, that is:

\llbracket 0\rrbracket=0

(iii)

\mathsf{F}_{\oc}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
preserves the additive structure strictly, that is, the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f+g)\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)+\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(g)\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(0)\rrbracket=0

(iv)

The following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\llbracket\iota_{0}\rrbracket=\iota_{0}\circ\varepsilon_{A}=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\iota_{0})\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\iota_{1}\rrbracket=\iota_{1}\circ\varepsilon_{B}=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\iota_{1})\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\nabla_{A}\rrbracket=\nabla_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A\times A}=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\nabla_{A})\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\ell_{A}\rrbracket=\ell_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A\times A}=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\ell_{A})\rrbracket

\llbracket c_{A}\rrbracket=c_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{(A\times A)\times(A\times A)}=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(c_{A})\rrbracket

where
\iota_{j}
,
\nabla
,
\ell
, and
c
are defined as in Definition
2.2
.

Now since every category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts is a Cartesian left additive category, it follows that for every comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
on
\mathbb{X}
, the coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
is a Cartesian left additive category. It is important to point out that even if all maps in
\mathbb{X}
are additive maps, the same is not true for
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
. This is due to the fact that
\oc(f+g)
and
\oc(0)
do not necessarily equal
\oc(f)+\oc(g)
and
0
respectively.

We now provide the first main result of this paper: that the coKleisli category of a Cartesian differential comonad is a Cartesian differential category.

Theorem 3.5

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
be a Cartesian differential comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts. Then the coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
is a Cartesian differential category where the Cartesian left additive structure is defined as in Lemma
3.4
and the differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
is defined as follows: for a map
{\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
, its derivative
\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket:\oc(A\times A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is defined as the following composite:

\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 20.4707pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.45836pt\raise 5.71251pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.6625pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 80.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 123.19246pt\raise 5.83499pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\llbracket f\rrbracket}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 163.17056pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 163.17056pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Furthermore:

(i)

For every object
A
in
\mathbb{X}
,
\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket=\partial_{A}
.

(ii)

A coKleisli map
\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear in
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
if and only if
\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})=\llbracket f\rrbracket
.

(iii)

For every map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in
\mathbb{X}
,
\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear in
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
.

(iv)

There is a functor
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]
which is defined on objects as
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}(A)=A
and on maps
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
as
\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}(f)\rrbracket=f\circ\varepsilon_{A}=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket
, and such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}_{\oc}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}_{\oc}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{U}}

Proof:
We prove the seven axioms of a differential combinator. We make heavy use of Lemma
3.2
.(
i
).

[CD.1]

Here we use the additive enrichment of
\mathbb{X}
:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f+g]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket f+g\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket+\llbracket g\rrbracket\right)\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}+\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{D}[g]\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{D}[0]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket 0\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=0\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=0

[CD.2]

Here we use the fact that every map in
\mathbb{X}
is additive, and both the zero rule
[dc.1]
and additive rule
[dc.2]
:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\circ(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A})\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A})\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.3
.(
vi
) + Lem.
3.4
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A})

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\left(\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})\right)

(
[dc.2]
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.3
.(
vi
) + Lem.
3.4
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\mathsf{D}[f]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\iota_{0}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\iota_{0})\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.4
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket\circ\oc(\iota_{0})

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{0})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ 0

(
[dc.1]
)

\displaystyle=~0

[CD.3]

Here we use the linear rule
[dc.3]
and Lemma
3.2
.(
ii
):

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{D}[1_{A}]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket 1_{A}\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\varepsilon_{A}\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\pi_{1}\circ\varepsilon_{A\times A}

(
[dc.3]
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\pi_{1}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\pi_{j}]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\pi_{0}\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A\times B}

\displaystyle=~\pi_{j}\circ\varepsilon_{A\times B}\circ\partial_{A\times B}

\displaystyle=~\pi_{j}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\varepsilon_{(A\times B)\times(A\times B)}

(
[dc.3]
)

\displaystyle=~\pi_{j}\circ\llbracket\pi_{1}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\pi_{j})\circ\pi_{1}\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.2
.(
ii
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\pi_{j}\circ\pi_{1}\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.3
.(
vi
))

[CD.4]

This is mostly straightforward from the product structure:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\langle f,g\rangle\right]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\langle f,g\rangle\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\left\langle\llbracket f\rrbracket,\llbracket g\rrbracket\right\rangle\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\left\langle\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A},\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=~\left\langle\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{D}[g]\rrbracket\right\rangle

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\langle f,g\rangle]\rrbracket

[CD.5]

Here we use the chain rule
[dc.4]
and the naturality of
\partial
:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{D}[g\circ f]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\circ f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\delta_{A}\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\langle\oc(\pi_{0}),\partial_{A}\rangle\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

(
[dc.4]
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\partial_{B}\circ\oc\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\times\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\oc\left(\langle\oc(\pi_{0}),\partial_{A}\rangle\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

(Naturality of
\partial
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[g]\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\times\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\oc\left(\langle\oc(\pi_{0}),\partial_{A}\rangle\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[g]\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\times\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\langle\oc(\pi_{0}),\partial_{A}\rangle\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

(Functoriality of
\oc
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[g]\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\oc(\pi_{0}),\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\right\rangle\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[g]\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\oc(\pi_{0}),\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket\right\rangle\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[g]\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\llbracket f\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\pi_{0})\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket\right\rangle\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[g]\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\llbracket f\circ\pi_{0}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket\right\rangle\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

(Lem.
3.3
.(
vi
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[g]\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\left\llbracket\left\langle f\circ\pi_{0},\mathsf{D}[f]\right\rangle\right\rrbracket\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

\displaystyle=~\left\llbracket\mathsf{D}[g]\circ\left\langle f\circ\pi_{0},\mathsf{D}[f]\right\rangle\right\rrbracket

[CD.6]

Here we use the lifting rule
[dc.5]
:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\circ\ell_{A}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\ell_{A})\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.4
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\rrbracket\circ\oc(\ell_{A})

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A\times A}\circ\oc(\ell_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\partial_{A\times A}\circ\oc(\ell_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}

(
[dc.5]
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket

[CD.7]

Here we use the symmetry rule
[dc.6]
:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\circ c_{A}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(c_{A})\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.4
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\rrbracket\circ\oc(c_{A})

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A\times A}\circ\oc(c_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\partial_{A\times A}\circ\oc(c_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\partial_{A\times A}

(
[dc.6]
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A\times A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\rrbracket

So we conclude that
\mathsf{D}
is a differential combinator, and therefore that the coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
is a Cartesian differential category. Next we prove the remaining claims.

(i)

This is automatic by definition since:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\varphi_{A}\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~1_{\oc(A)}\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\partial_{A}

(ii)

By Lemma
2.5
.(
iii
),
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if and only if
\llbracket\mathsf{L}[f]\rrbracket=\llbracket f\rrbracket
. However, expanding the left hand side of the equality we have that:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{L}[f]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\iota_{1}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\iota_{1})\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.4
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

Therefore,
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if and only if
\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})=\llbracket f\rrbracket
.

(iii)

For any map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in
\mathbb{X}
, using the linear rule
[dc.3]
, naturality of
\varepsilon
, and the biproduct identities, we compute:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

\displaystyle=~f\circ\varepsilon_{A}\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

\displaystyle=~f\circ\pi_{1}\circ\varepsilon_{A\times A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

\displaystyle=~f\circ\pi_{1}\circ\iota_{1}\circ\varepsilon_{A}

(Naturality of
\varepsilon
)

\displaystyle=~f\circ\varepsilon_{A}

(Biproduct Identity)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket

Therefore, since
\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket
, by the above, it follows that
\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear.

(iv)

By the above,
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
is well-defined and is indeed a functor since
\mathsf{F}_{\oc}
is a functor. Furthermore, it is automatic by definition that
\mathsf{U}\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}=\mathsf{F}_{\oc}
.

\Box

We will now prove the converse of Theorem
3.5
by showing that a comonad whose coKleisli category is a Cartesian differential category is indeed a Cartesian differential comonad.

Proposition 3.6

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a category with finite biproducts and let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
be a comonad on
\mathbb{X}
. Suppose that the coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
is a Cartesian differential category with differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
such that:

(i)

The underlying Cartesian left additive structure of
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
is the one from Lemma
3.4
;

(ii)

For every map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in
\mathbb{X}
,
\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket
is a
\mathsf{D}
-linear map in
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
.

Define the natural transformation
\partial_{A}:\oc(A\times A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
as follows:

\begin{gathered}\partial_{A}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 20.4707pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.76013pt\raise 6.15pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 80.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(3)

Then
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
is a Cartesian differential comonad and furthermore for every coKleisli map
\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, the following diagram commutes (in
\mathbb{X}
):

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 20.4707pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.07095pt\raise-24.0169pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 85.0969pt\raise-28.81697pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.45836pt\raise 5.71251pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.6625pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 80.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 91.82063pt\raise-15.95557pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\llbracket f\rrbracket}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 91.82063pt\raise-24.3pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.08885pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 85.0969pt\raise-31.08885pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(4)

Proof:
We begin by proving (
4
) as it will be useful in other parts of the proof:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\varphi_{A}\right]\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.2
.(
iii
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\mathsf{D}\left[\varphi_{A}\right]\rrbracket

(
\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear and Lem
2.5
.(
v
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\varphi_{A}\right]\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.2
.(
ii
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}

So
\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket=\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}
. Next we prove that
\partial
is natural:

\displaystyle\partial_{B}\circ\oc(f\times f)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{B}]\rrbracket\circ\oc(f\times f)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{B}]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f\times f)\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{B}]\circ\left(\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\times\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\right)\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.3
.(
vi
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{B}\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)]\rrbracket

(
\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear and Lem
2.5
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\varphi_{B}\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}

(
4
)

\displaystyle=~\oc(f)\circ\partial_{A}

(Lem.
3.2
.(
iv
))

So
\partial
is a natural transformation. Next we show the six axioms of a differential combinator transformation:

[dc.1]

Here we use
[CD.2]
:

\displaystyle\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{0})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\circ\oc(\iota_{0})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\iota_{0})\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\iota_{0}\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.4
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket 0\rrbracket

(
[CD.2]
)

\displaystyle=~0

[dc.2]

Here we use
[CD.2]
:

\displaystyle\partial_{A}\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A})\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A})\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.3
.(
vi
) + Lem.
3.4
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})\rrbracket

(
[CD.2]
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.3
.(
vi
) + Lem.
3.4
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\partial_{A}\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\partial_{A}\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})

\displaystyle=~\partial_{A}\circ\left(\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})\right)

[dc.3]

Here we use
[CD.3]
:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{A}\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket 1_{A}\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[1_{A}]\rrbracket

(
4
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\pi_{1}\rrbracket

(
[CD.3]
)

\displaystyle=~\pi_{1}\circ\varepsilon_{A\times A}

[dc.4]

Here we use
[CD.5]
:

\displaystyle\delta_{A}\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\varphi_{\oc(A)}\circ\varphi_{A}\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}

(Lem.
3.2
.(
v
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\varphi_{\oc(A)}\circ\varphi_{A}\right]\rrbracket

(
4
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\varphi_{\oc(A)}\right]\circ\langle\varphi_{A}\circ\pi_{0},\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rangle\rrbracket

(
[CD.5]
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\varphi_{\oc(A)}\right]\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\llbracket\langle\varphi_{A}\circ\pi_{0},\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rangle\rrbracket\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

\displaystyle=~\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\llbracket\langle\varphi_{A}\circ\pi_{0},\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rangle\rrbracket\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

\displaystyle=~\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\llbracket\varphi_{A}\circ\pi_{0}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\right\rangle\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

\displaystyle=~\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\llbracket\varphi_{A}\circ\pi_{0}\rrbracket,\partial_{A}\right\rangle\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

\displaystyle=~\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\llbracket\varphi_{A}\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\pi_{0})\rrbracket,\partial_{A}\right\rangle\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

(Lem.
3.3
.(
vi
))

\displaystyle=~\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\oc(\pi_{0}),\partial_{A}\right\rangle\right)\circ\delta_{A\times A}

(Lem.
3.2
.(
iv
))

[dc.5]

Here we use
[CD.6]
:

\displaystyle\partial_{A}\circ\partial_{A\times A}\circ\oc(\ell_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A\times A}\circ\oc(\ell_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right]\rrbracket\circ\oc(\ell_{A})

(
4
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\ell_{A})\right]\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\ell_{A}\right]\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.4
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket

(
[CD.6]
)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{A}

[dc.6]

Here we use
[CD.7]
:

\displaystyle\partial_{A}\circ\partial_{A\times A}\circ\oc(c_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A\times A}\circ\oc(c_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right]\rrbracket\circ\oc(c_{A})

(
4
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(c_{A})\right]\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ c_{A}\right]\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.4
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right]\rrbracket

(
[CD.7]
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A\times A}\rrbracket

(
4
)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{A}\circ\partial_{A\times A}

So we conclude that
\partial
is a differential combinator transformation and therefore that
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
is a Cartesian differential comonad.

\Box

As a result, we obtain a bijective correspondence between differential combinator transformations and differential combinators.

Corollary 3.7

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a category with finite biproducts and let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
be a comonad on
\mathbb{X}
. Then the following are in bijective correspondence:

(i)

Differential combinator transformations
\partial
on
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)

(ii)

Differential combinators
\mathsf{D}
on the coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
with respect to the Cartesian left additive structure from Lemma
3.4
and such that for every map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in
\mathbb{X}
,
\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is a
\mathsf{D}
-linear map in
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
.

via the constructions of Theorem
3.5
and Proposition
3.6
.

Proof:
This follows immediately from Theorem
3.5
.(
i
) and (
4
).

\Box

We now turn our attention back to the
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps in the coKleisli category of a Cartesian differential comonad. Specifically, we wish to provide necessary and sufficient conditions for when the subcategory of
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps is isomorphic to the base category. Explicitly, we wish to study when
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]
as defined in Theorem
3.5
.(
iv
) is an isomorphism. The answer, as it turns out, is requiring that the comonad counit has a section.

Definition 3.8

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
be a Cartesian differential comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts. A
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit
on
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
is a natural transformation
\eta_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
such that the following diagrams commute:

[du.1]

Linear Rule:

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{A}}
\textstyle{A}

[du.2]

Linearization Rule:

\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\oc(\iota_{1})}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)}

where
\iota_{1}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A\times A
is defined as in Definition
2.2
.(
i
).

In other words, for every object
A
,
\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})
is a split idempotent via
\eta_{A}
and
\varepsilon_{A}
.

Our first observation is that
\mathsf{D}
-linear units are unique.

Lemma 3.9

For a Cartesian differential comonad, if a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit exists, then it is unique.

Proof:
Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
be a Cartesian differential comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts. Suppose that
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
are two
\mathsf{D}
-linear units on
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
. Combining the linear rule
[du.1]
and the linearization rule
[du.2]
, we compute:

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}_{A}

\displaystyle=~\eta^{\prime}_{A}\circ 1_{A}

\displaystyle=~\eta^{\prime}_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}\circ\eta_{A}

(
[du.1]
for
\eta
)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})\circ\eta_{A}

(
[du.2]
for
\eta^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle=~\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}\circ\eta_{A}

(
[du.2]
for
\eta
)

\displaystyle=~\eta_{A}\circ 1_{A}

(
[du.1]
for
\eta
)

\displaystyle=~\eta_{A}

So
\eta=\eta^{\prime}
. Therefore we conclude that if it exists, a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit must be unique.
\Box

We now prove that for a Cartesian differential comonad with a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit, the
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps in the coKleisli category correspond precisely to the maps in the base category. To do so, we will first need the following useful identity:

Lemma 3.10

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
be a Cartesian differential comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts. Then
\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket=\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})
.

Proof:
We compute:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\iota_{1}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(\oc(\iota_{1})\right)\rrbracket

(Lem.
3.4
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

(Theorem
3.5
.(
i
))

So the desired equality holds.

\Box

Proposition 3.11

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
be a Cartesian differential comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]
is an isomorphism (where
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
is defined as in Theorem
3.5
.(
iv
));

(ii)

(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
has a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit
\eta_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
.

Proof:
Suppose that
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]
is an isomorphism. By Lemma
2.5
.(
ii
),
\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
is a
\mathsf{D}
-linear map from
A
to
\oc(A)
in the coKleisli category. Thus, we obtain a map of the desired type
{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\right):A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)}
in
\mathbb{X}
. So define
{\eta_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)}
as:

\begin{gathered}\eta_{A}=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\right)\end{gathered}

(5)

We will use Lemma
3.10
to show that
\eta
is indeed a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit. Starting with the naturality of
\eta
, for any map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in
\mathbb{X}
we compute:

\displaystyle\eta_{B}\circ f

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{B}]\rrbracket\right)\circ f

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{B}]\rrbracket\right)\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(f\right)\right)

(
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
is an isomorphism)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\left\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{B}]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(f\right)\right\rrbracket\right)

(
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\left\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{B}]\circ\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(f\right)\right\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{B}]\rrbracket\circ\oc(f)\right)

(Lem.
3.2
.(
i
))

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\partial_{B}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})\circ\oc(f)\right)

(Lemma
3.10
)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\partial_{B}\circ\oc\left(\iota_{1}\circ f\right)\right)

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\partial_{B}\circ\oc\left((f\times f)\circ\iota_{1}\right)\right)

(Naturality of
\iota_{1}
)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\partial_{B}\circ\oc(f\times f)\circ\oc(\iota_{1})\right)

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\oc(f)\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})\right)

(Naturality of
\partial
)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\oc(f)\circ\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\right)

(Lemma
3.10
)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\oc(f)\right)\circ\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\right)

(Lem.
3.2
.(
ii
))

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\left\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(\oc(f)\right)\circ\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\right\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(\oc(f)\right)\right)\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\right)

(
\mathsf{F}^{-1}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\oc(f)\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\right)

(
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
is an isomorphism)

\displaystyle=~\oc(f)\circ\eta_{A}

So
\eta
is a natural transformation. Next we show the two axioms of a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit:

[du.1]

We compute:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{A}\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(\varepsilon_{A}\right)\rrbracket\right)\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\varepsilon_{A}\right)\rrbracket\right)\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\llbracket 1_{A}\rrbracket\right)\rrbracket\right)\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\llbracket 1_{A}\rrbracket\right)\rrbracket\right)\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\llbracket 1_{A}\rrbracket\right)\circ\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\llbracket 1_{A}\rrbracket\right)\circ\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\iota_{1}\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\llbracket 1_{A}\rrbracket\right)\circ\varphi_{A}\right]\circ\iota_{1}\rrbracket\right)

(
\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\llbracket\varepsilon\rrbracket\right)
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear and Lem
2.5
.(
v
))

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{D}[1_{A}]\circ\iota_{1}\rrbracket\right)

(Lem.
3.2
.(
iii
))

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\pi_{1}\circ\iota_{1}\rrbracket\right)

(
[CD.3]
)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket 1_{A}\rrbracket\right)

(Biproduct identities)

\displaystyle=~1_{A}

(
\mathsf{F}^{-1}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
is a functor)

[du.2]

We compute:

\displaystyle\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(\eta_{A}\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket

(
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
is an isomorphism)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

(Lemma
3.10
)

So we conclude that
\eta
is a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit. Conversely, suppose that
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
comes equipped with a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit
\eta_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
. Define the functor
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}:\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
on objects as
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}(A)=A
and on
\mathsf{D}
-linear coKleisli maps
\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
as the following composite:

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}(\llbracket f\rrbracket):=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.502pt\raise 4.96875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.74374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 109.18977pt\raise 5.83499pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\llbracket f\rrbracket}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 149.16788pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 149.16788pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}(\llbracket f\rrbracket)=\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}

We must show that
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}
is indeed a functor. We first show that
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}
preserves identities using the linear rule
[du.1]
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket 1_{A}\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket 1_{A}\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~\varepsilon_{A}\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~1_{A}

(
[du.1]
)

Next we show that
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}
also preserves composition using the linearization rule
[du.2]
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket g\circ f\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\circ f\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\delta_{A}\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})\right)\circ\delta_{A}\circ\eta_{A}

(
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear and Thm.
3.5
.(
ii
))

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}\right)\circ\delta_{A}\circ\eta_{A}

(
[du.2]
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\oc(\eta_{A})\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A})\circ\delta_{A}\circ\eta_{A}

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\oc(\eta_{A})\circ 1_{\oc(A)}\circ\eta_{A}

(Comonad Identity)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\oc(\eta_{A})\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\oc\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\eta_{\oc(A)}\circ\eta_{A}

(Naturality of
\eta
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\eta_{B}\circ\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}

(Naturality of
\eta
)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket g\rrbracket\right)\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)

So
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}
is indeed a functor. Next we show that
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
and
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}
are inverses. Starting with
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
, clearly on objects we have that
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}(A)\right)=A
, while for maps we compute:

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}(f)\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}(f)\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~f\circ\varepsilon_{A}\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~f\circ 1_{A}

(
[du.1]
)

\displaystyle=~f

Therefore,
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{-1}\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}=1_{\mathbb{X}}
. Next for
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\circ\mathsf{F}^{-1}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
, clearly on objects we have that
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(\mathsf{F}^{-1}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}(A)\right)=A
, while for maps we compute:

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(\mathsf{F}^{-1}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{F}^{-1}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\circ\varepsilon_{A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

(
[du.2]
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket

(
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear and Thm.
3.5
.(
ii
))

Therefore,
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\circ\mathsf{F}^{-1}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}=1_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]}
. So we conclude that
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
is an isomorphism.

\Box

As a result, in the presence of a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit, we obtain the following characterizations of
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps.

Corollary 3.12

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
be a Cartesian differential comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts. If
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
has a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit
\eta
, then the following are equivalent for a coKleisli map
\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
,

(i)

\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear in
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}

(ii)

There exists a (necessarily unique) map
g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in
\mathbb{X}
such that
\llbracket f\rrbracket=g\circ\varepsilon_{A}=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(g)\rrbracket
.

(iii)

\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}=\llbracket f\rrbracket

Proof:
For
(i)\Rightarrow(ii)
, suppose that
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear. Then set
g=\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}=\mathsf{F}^{-1}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}(\llbracket f\rrbracket)
. By Proposition
3.11
, we clearly have that
\llbracket f\rrbracket=g\circ\varepsilon_{A}=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(g)\rrbracket
, and also that
g
is unique. For
(ii)\Rightarrow(iii)
, suppose that
\llbracket f\rrbracket=g\circ\varepsilon_{A}
. Then by
[du.1]
, we have that:

\displaystyle\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}

\displaystyle=~g\circ\varepsilon_{A}\circ\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}

\displaystyle=~g\circ 1_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}

(
[du.1]
)

\displaystyle=~g\circ\varepsilon_{A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket

Lastly, for
(iii)\Rightarrow(i)
, suppose that
\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}=\llbracket f\rrbracket
. By
[du.2]
, this implies that
\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})=\llbracket f\rrbracket
. However by Theorem
3.5
.(
ii
), this implies that
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear.

\Box

We conclude with some examples of Cartesian differential comonads and, if it exists, their
\mathsf{D}
-linear units.

Example 3.13

The main example of a Cartesian differential comonad is the comonad of a differential category. Briefly, a differential category
[
4
, Definition 2.4]
is an additive symmetric monoidal category
\mathbb{X}
equipped with a comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
, two natural transformations
\Delta_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)\otimes\oc(A)
and
e_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}I
such that
\oc(A)
is a cocommutative comonoid, and a natural transformation called a deriving transformation
\mathsf{d}_{A}:\oc(A)\otimes A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
satisfying certain coherences which capture the basic properties of differentiation
[
3
, Definition 7]
. By
[
5
, Proposition 3.2.1]
, for a differential category
\mathbb{X}
with finite products, its coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
is a Cartesian differential category where the differential combinator is defined using the deriving transformation. For a coKleisli map
\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, its derivative
\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket:\oc(A\times A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is defined as:

\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern Applying Proposition
3.6
, we obtain a differential combinator transformation:

\partial_{A}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern Furthermore, if there exists a natural transformation
u_{A}:I\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
such that
e_{A}\circ u_{A}=1_{I}
and
u_{A}\circ e_{A}=\oc(0)
, then we obtain a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit defined as follows:

\eta_{A}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 219.83383pt\raise 5.71251pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.6625pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}_{A}}$}}}\kern 256.39883pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Readers familiar with differential linear logic will note that any differential
storage
category
[
4
, Definition 4.10]
has such a map
u
and that in this case the
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit is precisely the codereliction
[
3
, Section 5]
. However, we stress that it is possible to have a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit for differential categories that are not differential storage categories. We invite the reader to see
[
3
, Section 9]
and
[
25
, Example 4.7]
for lists of examples of differential categories.

Example 3.14

Our three main novel examples of Cartesian differential comonads that we introduce in Sections
5
,
6
, and
7
below, arise instead more naturally as the dual notion, which we simply call
coCartesian differential monads
. Following the convention in the differential category literature for the dual notion of differential categories, we have elected to keep the same terminology and notation for the dual notion of a differential combinator transformation. Briefly, a coCartesian differential monad on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts is a quadruple
(\mathsf{S},\mu,\eta,\partial)
consisting of a monad
(\mathsf{S},\mu,\eta)
(where
{\mu_{A}:\mathsf{S}\mathsf{S}(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{S}(A)}
and
\eta_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{S}(A)
) and a natural transformation
\partial_{A}:\mathsf{S}(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{S}(A\times A)
, again called a differential combinator transformation, such that the dual diagrams of Definition
3.1
commute. By the dual statement of Proposition
3.6
, the opposite category of the Kleisli category of a coCartesian differential monad is a Cartesian differential category. The dual notion of a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit is called a
\mathsf{D}
-linear counit, which would be a natural transformation
\varepsilon_{A}:\mathsf{S}(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that the dual diagrams of Definition
3.8
commute. By the dual statement of Proposition
3.11
, the existence of a
\mathsf{D}
-linear counit implies that the opposite of the base category is isomorphic to the subcategory of the
\mathsf{D}
-linear of the opposite of the Kleisli category.

The following are two “trivial” examples of Cartesian differential categories any category with finite biproducts. While both are “trivial” in their own way, they both provide simple separating examples. Indeed, the first is an example of a Cartesian differential comonad without a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit, while the second is a Cartesian differential comonad which is not induced by a differential category.

Example 3.15

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a category with finite biproducts, and let
\top
be the chosen zero object. Then the constant comonad
\mathsf{C}
which sends every object to the zero object
\mathsf{C}(A)=\top
and every map to zero maps
\mathsf{C}(f)=0
is a Cartesian differential comonad whose differential combinator transformation is simply
0
. This Cartesian differential comonad has a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit if and only if every object of
\mathbb{X}
is a zero object.

Example 3.16

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a category with finite biproducts. Then the identity comonad
1_{\mathbb{X}}
is a Cartesian differential comonad whose differential combinator transformation is the second projection
\pi_{1}:A\times A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and has a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit given by the identity map
1_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
. The resulting coKleisli category is simply the entire base category
\mathbb{X}
and whose differential combinator the same as in Example
2.6
. As such, this example recaptures Example
2.6
that every category with finite biproducts is a Cartesian differential category where every map is
\mathsf{D}
-linear.

4
Cartesian Differential Abstract coKleisli Categories

The goal of this section is to give a precise characterization of the Cartesian differential categories which are the coKleisli categories of Cartesian differential comonads. This is a generalization of the work done by Blute, Cockett, and Seely in
[
6
]
, where they characterize which Cartesian differential categories are the coKleisli categories of the comonads of differential categories. This was achieved using the concept of abstract coKleisli categories
[
6
, Section 2.4]
, which is the dual notion of thunk-force-categories as introduced by Führmann in
[
24
]
. Abstract coKleisli categories provide a direct description of the structure of coKleisli categories in such a way that the coKleisli category of a comonad is an abstract coKleisli category and, conversely, every abstract coKleisli category is canonically the coKleisli category of a comonad on a certain subcategory. As such, here we introduced Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli categories which, as the name suggests, are abstract coKleisli categories that are also Cartesian differential categories such that the differential combinator and abstract coKleisli structure are compatible. We show that the coKleisli category of a Cartesian differential comonad is a Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli categories and that, conversely, every Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli category is canonically the coKleisli category of a Cartesian differential comonad on a certain subcategory. We will also study the
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps of Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli categories.

We will start from the abstract coKleisli side of the story.

Definition 4.1

An
abstract coKleisli structure
on a category
\mathbb{X}
is a triple
(\oc,\varphi,\epsilon)
consisting of an endofunctor
\oc:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
, a natural transformation
\varphi_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
, and a family of maps
\epsilon_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
(which are not necessarily natural), such that
\epsilon_{\oc(A)}:\oc\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
is a natural transformation and the following diagrams commute:

(6)

An
abstract coKleisli category

[
6
, Definition 2.4.1]
is a category
\mathbb{X}
equipped with an abstract coKleisli structure
(\oc,\varphi,\epsilon)
.

Below in Lemma
4.12
, we will review how every coKleisli category is an abstract coKleisli category. In order to obtain the converse, we first need from an abstract coKleisli category to construct a category with comonad. In an abstract coKleisli category, there are an important class of maps called the
\epsilon
-natural maps (which are the dual of thunkable maps in thunk-force categories
[
24
, Definition 7]
). These
\epsilon
-natural maps form a subcategory which comes equipped with a comonad, and the coKleisli category of this comonad is the starting abstract coKleisli category.

Definition 4.2

In an abstract coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}
with abstract coKleisli structure
(\oc,\varphi,\epsilon)
, a map
{f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
is said to
\epsilon
-natural
if the following diagram commutes:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern
