(7)

Define the subcategory of
\epsilon
-natural maps
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]
to be the category whose objects are the same as
\mathbb{X}
and whose maps are
\epsilon
-natural in
\mathbb{X}
, and let
\mathsf{U}_{\epsilon}:\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
be the obvious forgetful functor.

As we will discuss in Lemma
4.13
, in the context of a coKleisli category of a comonad, these
\epsilon
-natural maps should be thought of as the maps in the base category. Here are now some basic properties of
\epsilon
-natural maps.

Lemma 4.3

[
6
, Section 2.4]
Let
\mathbb{X}
be an abstract coKleisli category with abstract coKleisli structure
(\oc,\varphi,\epsilon)
. Then:

(i)

Identity maps
1_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
are
\epsilon
-natural;

(ii)

If
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
g:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
are
\epsilon
-natural, then their composite
g\circ f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
is
\epsilon
-natural;

(iii)

For every object
A
,
\epsilon_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is
\epsilon
-natural;

(iv)

For every map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
,
\oc(f):\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(B)
is
\epsilon
-natural.

We now review in detail how every abstract coKleisli category is isomorphic to the coKleisli category of a canonical comonad on the subcategory of
\epsilon
-natural maps.

Lemma 4.4

[
24
, Dual of Theorem 4]
Let
\mathbb{X}
be an abstract coKleisli category with abstract coKleisli structure
(\oc,\varphi,\epsilon)
. Define the natural transformation
\beta_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc\oc(A)
as follows:

\beta_{A}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 11.34993pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.71439pt\raise 6.15pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\oc(\varphi_{A})}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 71.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc\oc(A)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Then
(\oc,\beta,\epsilon)
is a comonad on
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]
such that the functor
\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]_{\oc}
defined on objects as
\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}(A)=A
and on a map
{f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
as the following composite:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}(f)\rrbracket:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 11.34993pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.71216pt\raise 6.15pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\oc(f)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 47.34993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 81.84344pt\raise 4.88124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.83124pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{B}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 106.56123pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 106.56123pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}(f)\rrbracket=\epsilon_{B}\circ\oc(f)

is an isomorphism with inverse
\mathsf{G}^{-1}_{\epsilon}:\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]_{\oc}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
defined on objects as
\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}(A)=A
and on a coKleisli map
{\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
as the following composite:

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{-1}_{\epsilon}\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right):=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.97408pt\raise 4.96875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.74374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varphi_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 42.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.18977pt\raise 5.83499pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\llbracket f\rrbracket}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 101.16788pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 101.16788pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{-1}_{\epsilon}\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)=\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\varphi_{A}

We now wish to equip abstract coKleisli categories with Cartesian differential structure. To do so, we must first discuss Cartesian left additive structure for abstract coKleisli categories. We start with the finite product structure:

Definition 4.5

A
Cartesian abstract coKleisli category

[
6
, Definition 2.4.1]
is an abstract coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}
with abstract coKleisli structure
(\oc,\varphi,\epsilon)
such that
\mathbb{X}
has finite products and all the projection maps
\pi_{0}:A\times B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
\pi_{1}:A\times B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
are
\epsilon
-natural.

For a Cartesian abstract coKleisli category, it follows that
\epsilon
-natural maps are closed under the finite product structure.

Lemma 4.6

[
6
, Section 2.4]
Let
\mathbb{X}
be a Cartesian abstract coKleisli category with abstract coKleisli structure
(\oc,\varphi,\epsilon)
. Then:

(i)

If
f:C\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
g:C\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
are
\epsilon
-natural, then their pairing
\langle f,g\rangle:C\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A\times B
is
\epsilon
-natural;

(ii)

If
h:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
and
k:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}D
are
\epsilon
-natural, then their product
h\times k:A\times B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C\times D
is
\epsilon
-natural.

Therefore,
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]
has finite products (which is defined as in
\mathbb{X}
).

Next we discuss Cartesian left additive structure for abstract coKleisli categories, where we require that
\epsilon
-natural maps are closed under the additive structure.

Definition 4.7

A
Cartesian left additive abstract coKleisli category
is a Cartesian abstract coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}
with abstract coKleisli structure
(\oc,\varphi,\epsilon)
such that
\mathbb{X}
is also a Cartesian left additive category, zero maps
0:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
are
\epsilon
-natural, and if
{f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
and
g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
are
\epsilon
-natural, then their sum
{f+g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
is
\epsilon
-natural.

For a Cartesian left additive abstract coKleisli category, the subcategory of
\epsilon
-natural maps also form a Cartesian left additive category. It is important to stress however that
\epsilon
-natural maps are not assumed to be additive, and therefore the subcategory of
\epsilon
-natural maps does not necessarily have biproducts.

Lemma 4.8

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a Cartesian left additive abstract coKleisli category with abstract coKleisli structure
(\oc,\varphi,\epsilon)
. Then
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]
is a Cartesian left additive category (where the necessary structure is defined as in
\mathbb{X}
). Furthermore,

(i)

\epsilon_{A}\circ\oc(0)=0

(ii)

If
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
are
\epsilon
-natural, then
\varepsilon_{B}\circ\oc(f+g)=\epsilon_{B}\circ\oc(f)+\epsilon_{B}\circ\oc(g)

Proof:
It is clear that
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]
is a Cartesian left additive category. For (
i
) we use the fact that
0
is
\epsilon
-natural:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{A}\circ\oc(0)

\displaystyle=~0\circ\epsilon_{A}

(
0
is
\epsilon
-natual)

\displaystyle=~0

For (
ii
), we use the fact that the sum of
\epsilon
-natural maps is
\epsilon
-natural:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{B}\circ\oc(f+g)

\displaystyle=~(f+g)\circ\epsilon_{A}

(
f+g
is
\epsilon
-natual)

\displaystyle=~f\circ\epsilon_{A}+g\circ\epsilon_{A}

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{B}\circ\oc(f)+\epsilon_{B}\circ\oc(g)

(
f
and
g
are
\epsilon
-natural)

\Box

We are now in a position to define Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli categories.

Definition 4.9

A
Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli category
is a Cartesian differential category
\mathbb{X}
, with differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
, such that
\mathbb{X}
is also a Cartesian left additive abstract coKleisli category with abstract coKleisli structure
(\oc,\varphi,\epsilon)
and every
\epsilon
-natural map is
\mathsf{D}
-linear.

We will now show that for a Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli category, the canonical comonad on the subcategory of
\epsilon
-natural maps is a Cartesian differential comonad and that the coKleisli category is isomorphic to the starting Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli category.

Proposition 4.10

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli category with differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
and abstract coKleisli structure
(\oc,\varphi,\epsilon)
. Then
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]
is a category with finite biproducts and
(\oc,\beta,\epsilon,\partial)
(where
(\oc,\beta,\epsilon)
is defined as in Lemma
4.4
) is a Cartesian differential comonad on
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]
where the differential combinator transformation
\partial_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A\times A)
is defined as follows:

\begin{gathered}\partial_{A}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 20.4707pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A\times A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.34352pt\raise 6.15pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 80.4707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 126.77922pt\raise 5.52124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.19124pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{\oc(A)}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 165.73999pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 165.73999pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(8)

Furthermore,
\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]_{\oc}
is a Cartesian differential isomorphism, so in particular, the following equalities hold:

\begin{gathered}\llbracket\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}(\mathsf{D}[f])\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}(f)\right]\rrbracket\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\mathsf{G}^{-1}_{\epsilon}\left(\llbracket\mathsf{D}[f]\rrbracket\right)=\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{G}^{-1}_{\epsilon}\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\right)\right]\end{gathered}

(9)

where the differential combinator on the coKleisli category
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]_{\oc}
is defined as in Theorem
3.5
.

Proof:
We must first explain why
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]
has finite biproducts. By Lemma
4.8
,
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]
is a Cartesian left additive category. However, by assumption, every
\epsilon
-natural map is
\mathsf{D}
-linear, so by Lemma
2.5
.(
i
), every
\epsilon
-natural map is also additive. Therefore,
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]
is a Cartesian left additive category such that every map is additive, and so we conclude that
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]
has finite biproducts.

Next we must explain why the proposed differential combinator transformation is well defined, that is, we must show that
\partial_{A}
is indeed
\epsilon
-natural. However, by Lemma
4.3
.(
iii
) and (
iv
),
\epsilon_{\oc A}
and
\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)
are both
\epsilon
-natural. Then by Lemma
4.3
.(
ii
), their composite
\partial_{A}=\epsilon_{\oc A}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)
is
\epsilon
-natural. Next we show the naturality of
\partial
. So for an
\epsilon
-natural map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, we compute:

\displaystyle\partial_{B}\circ\oc(f\times f)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(B)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{B}]\right)\circ\oc(f\times f)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(B)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{B}]\circ(f\times f)\right)

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(B)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{B}\circ f]\right)

(
f
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear and Lem
2.5
.(
iv
))

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(B)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\oc(f)\circ\varphi_{A}]\right)

(Naturality of
f
)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(B)}\circ\oc\left(\oc(f)\circ\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)

(
\oc(f)
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear and Lem
2.5
.(
v
))

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(B)}\circ\oc\oc(f)\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\oc(f)\circ\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)

(Naturality of
\epsilon_{\oc(-)}
)

\displaystyle=~\oc(f)\circ\partial_{A}

So
\partial
is a natural transformation. Now we must show that
\partial
satisfies the six axioms of a differential combinator transformation. Note that the calculations below are similar to the ones in the proof of Proposition
3.6
.

[dc.1]

Here we use
[CD.2]
:

\displaystyle\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{0})

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)\circ\oc(\iota_{0})

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\iota_{0}\right)

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc(0)

(
[CD.2]
)

\displaystyle=~0

(Lem.
4.8
.(
i
))

[dc.2]

Here we use
[CD.2]
:

\displaystyle\partial_{A}\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A})

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A})

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ(1_{A}\times\nabla_{A})\right)

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})\right)

(
[CD.2]
)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})\right)+\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})\right)

(Lem.
4.8
.(
ii
))

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)\circ\oc\left(1_{A}\times\pi_{0}\right)+\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)\circ\oc\left(1_{A}\times\pi_{1}\right)

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{A}\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\partial_{A}\circ\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{1})

\displaystyle=~\partial_{A}\circ\left(\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})+\oc(1_{A}\times\pi_{0})\right)

[dc.3]

Here we use
[CD.3]
and the fact that since
\epsilon_{A}
is
\epsilon
-natural, that it is also
\mathsf{D}
-linear:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{A}\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{A}\circ\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{A}\circ\oc(\epsilon_{A})\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)

(Abstract coKleisli structure identity)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{A}\circ\oc\left(\epsilon_{A}\circ\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{A}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}\left[\epsilon_{A}\circ\varphi_{A}\right]\right)

(
\epsilon
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear and Lem
2.5
.(
v
))

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{A}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}\left[1_{A}\right]\right)

(Abstract coKleisli structure identity)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{A}\circ\oc\left(\pi_{1}\right)

(
[CD.3]
)

\displaystyle=~\pi_{1}\circ\epsilon_{A\times A}

(
\pi_{1}
is
\epsilon
-natural)

[dc.4]

Here we use
[CD.5]
:

\displaystyle\beta_{A}\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\oc(\varphi_{A})\circ\partial_{A}

\displaystyle=~\oc(\varphi_{A})\circ\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc\oc(A)}\circ\oc\oc(\varphi_{A})\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)

(Naturality of
\epsilon_{\oc(-)}
)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\oc(\varphi_{A})\circ\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}\left[\oc(\varphi_{A})\circ\varphi_{A}\right]\right)

(
\oc(\varphi_{A})
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear and Lem
2.5
.(
v
))

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}\left[\varphi_{\oc(A)}\circ\varphi_{A}\right]\right)

(Naturality of
\varphi
)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}\left[\varphi_{\oc(A)}\right]\circ\left\langle\varphi_{A}\circ\pi_{0},\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right\rangle\right)

(
[CD.5]
)

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}\left[\varphi_{\oc(A)}\right]\right)\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\varphi_{A}\circ\pi_{0},\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right\rangle\right)

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\varphi_{A}\circ\pi_{0},\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\oc(\pi_{0})\circ\varphi_{A\times A},\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right\rangle\right)

(Naturality of
\varphi
)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\oc(\pi_{0})\circ\varphi_{A\times A},1_{\oc(A)}\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\oc(\pi_{0})\circ\varphi_{A\times A},\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\varphi_{\oc(A)}\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right\rangle\right)

(Abstract coKleisli structure identity)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\left\langle\oc(\pi_{0})\circ\varphi_{A\times A},\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right)\circ\varphi_{A\times A}\right\rangle\right)

(Naturality of
\varphi
)

\begin{gathered}\partial_{A}\circ\partial_{A\times A}=\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc\left(\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\right]\right)\end{gathered}

\varphi

\Box

It is important to note that while
\epsilon
-natural maps are assumed to be
\mathsf{D}
-linear, the converse is not necessarily true. It turns out that all
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps are
\epsilon
-natural precisely when the Cartesian differential comonad has a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit.

Lemma 4.11

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli category with differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
and abstract coKleisli structure
(\oc,\varphi,\epsilon)
. Define the natural transformation
\eta_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
as follows:

\begin{gathered}\eta_{A}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.0512pt\raise 6.15pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(11)

where
\mathsf{L}
is defined as in Lemma
2.5
.(
ii
). Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]=\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}]
, that is, every
\mathsf{D}
-linear map is
\epsilon
-natural;

(ii)

For every object

,
\eta_{A}
is
\epsilon
-natural;

(iii)

\eta
is a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit for
(\oc,\beta,\epsilon,\partial)
.

Proof:
We first show that
\eta
is indeed a natural transformation. So for a
\epsilon
-natural map

, we compute:

\displaystyle\oc(f)\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~\oc(f)\circ\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]

\displaystyle=~\oc(f)\circ\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\iota_{1}

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{D}[\oc(f)\circ\varphi_{A}]\circ\iota_{1}

(
\oc(f)
is
\mathsf{D}

2.5
.(

))

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{B}\circ f]\circ\iota_{1}

(Naturality of
\varphi
)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{B}]\circ(f\times f)\circ\iota_{1}

(

is
\mathsf{D}
-linear and Lem
2.5
.(

))

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{B}]\circ\iota_{1}\circ f

\displaystyle=~\eta_{B}\circ f

So
\eta
is indeed a natural transformation.

For
(i)\Rightarrow(ii)
, first note that by definition, every
\epsilon
-natural map is
\mathsf{D}
-linear. So also assuming the converse that every
\mathsf{D}
-linear map is
\epsilon
-natural does indeed imply that
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]=\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}]
. Now suppose that this is the case. By Lemma
2.5
.(

),
\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear, and so by assumption,
\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]
is also
\epsilon
-natural. Next, for

, we must show that
\eta
satisfies both
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit axioms:

[du.1]

Here we use
[CD.3]
:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{A}\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{A}\circ\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{A}\circ\mathsf{D}[\varphi_{A}]\circ\iota_{1}

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{D}[\epsilon_{A}\circ\varphi_{A}]\circ\iota_{1}

(
\varepsilon
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear and Lem
2.5
.(

))

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{D}[1_{A}]\circ\iota_{1}

\displaystyle=~\pi_{1}\circ\iota_{1}

\displaystyle=~1_{A}

[du.2]

Here we use that
\eta_{A}
is assumed to be
\epsilon

\displaystyle\eta_{A}\circ\epsilon_{A}

\displaystyle=~\epsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc(\eta_{A})

(

\mathsf{D}

, we have that

. By Proposition

,
\mathbb{X}
and
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]_{\oc}
are isomorphic as Cartesian differential categories, which implies that we also have that
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}]\cong\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]_{\oc}\right]
. Therefore,

. However, it is straightforward to work out that this isomorphism is in fact an equality and so
\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}]=\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}]
.

\Box

\mathsf{F}_{\epsilon}(A)=A
and on maps
{f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
as
\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\epsilon}(f)\rrbracket=f\circ\varepsilon_{A}=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(f)\rrbracket
, and such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}_{\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}_{\oc}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}_{\oc}}
\textstyle{\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{U}}

A natural question to ask is when the subcategory of
\epsilon
-natural maps of a coKleisli category is isomorphic to the base category. The answer is when the comonad is exact (for monads, this is called the equalizer requirement
[
24
, Definition 8]
).

Lemma 4.13

[
24
, Dual of Theorem 9]
Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
be a comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathsf{F}_{\epsilon}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]
is an isomorphism;

(ii)

The comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
is exact
[
6
, Secion 2.6]
, that is, for every object
A
, the following is a coequalizer diagram:

\textstyle{\oc\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\oc(\varepsilon_{A})}
\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{A}}
\textstyle{A}

In the case of an exact comonad, the base category can be recovered from the coKleisli category using the subcategory of
\epsilon
-natural maps. For abstract coKleisli categories, note that the comonad from Lemma
4.4
is always exact.

For a comonad on the category with finite products, the coKleisli category is a Cartesian abstract coKleisli category.

Lemma 4.14

[
6
, Section 2.6]
Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
be a comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite products. Then the coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
is a Cartesian abstract coKleisli category with abstract coKleisli structure as defined in Lemma
4.12
.

For a comonad on a Cartesian left additive category, the coKleisli category is a Cartesian left additive abstract coKleisli category.

Lemma 4.15

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
be a comonad on a Cartesian left additive category
\mathbb{X}
. Then the coKleisli category
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
is a Cartesian left additive abstract coKleisli category with abstract coKleisli structure as defined in Lemma
4.12
and Cartesian left additive structure as defined in Lemma
3.4
.

Proof:
We must show that zero maps are
\epsilon
-natural, and that the sum of
\epsilon
-natural maps is again
\epsilon
-natural. We will make use of Lemma
4.12
.(
i
). Starting with zero maps, we compute:

\displaystyle\llbracket 0\rrbracket\circ\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}

\displaystyle=~0\circ\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}

\displaystyle=~0

\displaystyle=~0\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket 0\rrbracket\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A})

So
\llbracket 0\rrbracket\circ\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}=\llbracket 0\rrbracket\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A})
, so by Lemma
4.12
.(
i
), we conclude that
\llbracket 0\rrbracket
is
\epsilon
-natural. Next, suppose that
{\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
and
\llbracket g\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
are both
\epsilon
-natural, then we compute

\displaystyle\llbracket f+g\rrbracket\circ\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}

\displaystyle=~\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket+\llbracket g\rrbracket\right)\circ\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}+\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A})+\llbracket g\rrbracket\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A})

(
\llbracket f\rrbracket
and
\llbracket g\rrbracket
are
\epsilon
-nat. + Lem.
4.12
.(
i
) )

\displaystyle=~\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket+\llbracket g\rrbracket\right)\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f+g\rrbracket\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A})

So
\llbracket f+g\rrbracket\circ\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}=\llbracket f+g\rrbracket\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A})
, and so by Lemma
4.12
.(
i
) it follows that
\llbracket f+g\rrbracket
is
\epsilon
-natural. Therefore, we conclude that
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
is a Cartesian left additive abstract coKleisli category.

\Box

We will now show that for a Cartesian differential comonad, its coKleisli category is a Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli category.

Proposition 4.16

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
be a Cartesian differential comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts. Then
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
is a Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli category with Cartesian differential structure defined in Theorem
3.5
and abstract coKleisli structure
(\oc_{\oc},\varphi,\epsilon)
as defined in Lemma
4.12
.

Proof:
We must show that every
\epsilon
-natural map is
\mathsf{D}
-linear. To do so, we make use of both Theorem
3.5
.(
ii
) and Lemma
4.12
.(
i
). So suppose that
\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is
\epsilon
-natural, then we compute:

\displaystyle\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})\circ 1_{\oc(A)}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A})\circ\delta_{A}

(Comonad Identity)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1}\circ\varepsilon_{A})\circ\delta_{A}

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc\left((\varepsilon_{A}\times\varepsilon_{A})\circ\iota_{1}\right)\circ\delta_{A}

(Naturality of
\iota_{1}
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A}\times\varepsilon_{A})\circ\oc(\iota_{1})\circ\delta_{A}

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A})\circ\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})\circ\delta_{A}

(Naturality of
\partial
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\partial_{\oc(A)}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})\circ\delta_{A}

(
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\epsilon
-nat. + Lem.
4.12
.(
i
) )

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\pi_{1}\circ\varepsilon_{\oc(A)\times\oc(A)}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})\circ\delta_{A}

(
[dc.3]
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\pi_{1}\circ\iota_{1}\circ\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\delta_{A}

(Naturality of
\varepsilon
)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ 1_{\oc(A)}\circ 1_{\oc(A)}

(Biproduct Idenity + Comonad Identity)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket f\rrbracket

So
\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})=\llbracket f\rrbracket
, and so by Theorem
3.5
.(
ii
), it follows that
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear. Therefore, we conclude that
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
is a Cartesian differential abstract coKleisli category.

\Box

We conclude this section by showing that for a Cartesian differential comonad with a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit, the underlying comonad is exact and that a coKleisli map is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if and only if it
\epsilon
-natural.

Lemma 4.17

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
be a Cartesian differential comonad on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
has a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit
\eta_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
;

(ii)

The comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
is exact and for each object
A
, the
\mathsf{D}
-linear map
\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
is
\epsilon
-natural.

Proof:
For
(i)\Rightarrow(ii)
, suppose that
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
has a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit
\eta_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
. By the comonad identities, we have that
\delta_{A}
is a common section of
\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}
and
\oc(\varepsilon_{A})
, that is,
\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}\circ\delta_{A}=1_{\oc(A)}=\oc(\varepsilon_{A})\circ\delta_{A}
. By
[du.1]
, we also have that
\eta_{A}
is a section of
\varepsilon_{A}
, that is,
\varepsilon_{A}\circ\eta_{A}
. As such, this implies that the following diagram is a split coequalizer:

\textstyle{\oc\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\oc(\varepsilon_{A})}
\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{A}}
\textstyle{A}

However, every split coequalizer diagram is also an equalizer diagram. So we conclude that
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
is exact. Next we must show that
\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket
is
\epsilon
-natural. Note that as was shown in the proof of Proposition
3.11
,
\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket=\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}(\eta_{A})\rrbracket
. Then by Lemma
4.12
.(
ii
), it follows that
\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket
is
\epsilon
-natural.

For
(ii)\Rightarrow(i)
, suppose that
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
is exact and
\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket
is
\epsilon
-natural. By Lemma
4.12
.(
i
), the latter implies that
\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\circ\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}=\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket\circ\oc(\varepsilon_{A})
. Therefore, by the couniversal property of the coequalizer, there exists a unique map
\eta_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\oc\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{\oc(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\oc(\varepsilon_{A})}
\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{A}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists\oc~\eta_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)}

To show that
\eta
is a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit, we will make use of Lemma
3.10
and of the fact that
\varepsilon_{A}
is epic (which is a consequence of the coequalizer assumption). Starting with naturality, for any map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, we compute:

\displaystyle\oc(f)\circ\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}

\displaystyle=~\oc(f)\circ\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\oc(f)\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

(Lemma
3.10
)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{B}\circ\oc(f\times f)\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

(Naturality of
\partial
)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{B}\circ\oc\left((f\times f)\circ\iota_{1}\right)

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{B}\circ\oc(\iota_{1}\circ f)

(Naturality of
\iota_{1}
)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{B}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})\circ\oc(f)

(
\oc
is a functor)

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{B}]\rrbracket\circ\oc(f)

\displaystyle=~\eta_{B}\circ\varepsilon_{B}\circ\oc(f)

\displaystyle=~\eta_{B}\circ f\circ\varepsilon_{A}

(Naturality of
\varepsilon
)

So
\oc(f)\circ\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}=\eta_{B}\circ f\circ\varepsilon_{A}
. Since
\varepsilon_{A}
is epic, it follows that
\oc(f)\circ\eta_{A}=\eta_{B}\circ f
. Therefore,
\eta
is a natural transformation. Next we show that
\eta
satisfies the two axioms of a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit:

[du.1]

Here we use
[dc.3]
:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{A}\circ\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}

\displaystyle=~\varepsilon_{A}\circ\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\varepsilon_{A}\circ\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

(Lemma
3.10
)

\displaystyle=~\pi_{1}\circ\varepsilon_{A\times A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

(
[dc.3]
)

\displaystyle=~\pi_{1}\circ\iota_{1}\circ\varepsilon_{A}

(Naturality of
\varepsilon
)

\displaystyle=~1_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}

(Biproduct Identity)

\displaystyle=~\varepsilon_{A}

So
\varepsilon_{A}\circ\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}=\varepsilon_{A}
. Since
\varepsilon_{A}
is epic, it follows that
\varepsilon_{A}\circ\eta_{A}=1_{A}
.

[du.2]

Automatic by Lemma
3.10
since:

\displaystyle\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}

\displaystyle=~\llbracket\mathsf{L}[\varphi_{A}]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=~\partial_{A}\circ\oc(\iota_{1})

(Lemma
3.10
)

So we conclude that
\eta
is a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit.

\Box

Corollary 4.18

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\partial)
be a Cartesian differential comonad with a
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit
\eta
on a category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts. Then for a coKleisli map
\llbracket f\rrbracket:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
,
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear in
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
if and only if
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\epsilon
-natural in
\mathbb{X}_{\oc}
. As such, we have the following chain of isomorphisms:
\mathbb{X}\cong\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]\cong\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]

Proof:
By Proposition
4.16
, we already have that every
\epsilon
-natural map is
\mathsf{D}
-linear. So suppose that
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear. By Corollary
3.12
, since
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear, we have that
\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}\circ\varepsilon_{A}=\llbracket f\rrbracket
. However, this also implies that
\llbracket f\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{F}_{\oc}\left(\llbracket f\rrbracket\circ\eta_{A}\right)\rrbracket
. Then by Lemma
4.12
.(
ii
), it follows that
\llbracket f\rrbracket
is
\epsilon
-natural. By Proposition
3.11
, we have that
\mathbb{X}\cong\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]
, while by Lemma
4.17
and Lemma
4.13
, we have that
\mathbb{X}\cong\epsilon\text{-}\mathsf{nat}[\mathbb{X}_{\oc}]
.
\Box

5
Example: Reduced Power Series

In this section we construct a Cartesian differential comonad (in the opposite category) based on
reduced
formal power series, which therefore induces a Cartesian differential category of
reduced
formal power series. To the extent of the authors’ knowledge, this is a new observation. This is an interesting and important non-trivial example of a Cartesian differential comonad which does not arise from a differential category. Unsurprisingly, the differential combinator will reflect the standard differentiation of arbitrary multivariable power series. However, the problem with arbitrary power series lies with composition. Indeed, famously, power series with degree 0 coefficients, also called constant terms, cannot be composed, since in general this results in an infinite non-converging sum in the base field. Thus, multivariable formal power series do not form a category, since their composition may be undefined.
Reduced
formal power series are power series with no constant term. These can be composed
[
7
, Section 4.1]
and thus, we obtain a Lawvere theory of reduced power series. The total derivative of a reduced power series is again reduced, and therefore, we obtain a Cartesian differential category of reduced power series. Futhermore, this Cartesian differential category of reduced power series is in fact a subcategory of the opposite category of the Kleisli category of the coCartesian differential monad
\mathsf{P}
, the free reduced power series algebra monad, which can be seen as the free complete algebra functor induced by the operad of commutative algebras
[
22
, Section 1.4.4]
. Lastly, it is worth mentioning that, while in this section we will work with vector spaces over a field, we note that all the constructions easily generalize to the category of modules over a commutative (semi)ring.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a field. For an
\mathbb{F}
-vector space
V
, define
\mathsf{P}(V)
as follows:

\mathsf{P}(V)=\prod^{\infty}_{n=1}\left(V^{\otimes n}\right)_{S(n)},

where
(V^{\otimes n})_{S(n)}
denotes the vector space of symmetrized
n
-tensors, that is, classes of tensors of length
n
under the action of the symmetric group which permutes the factors in
V^{\otimes n}
. An arbitrary element
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathsf{P}(V)
is then an infinite ordered list
\mathfrak{t}=\left(\mathfrak{t}(n)\right)^{\infty}_{n=1}
where
\mathfrak{t}(n)\in(V^{\otimes n})_{S(n)}
. Therefore, an arbitrary element of
\mathsf{P}(V)
can be written in the following form:

\mathfrak{t}=\left(\mathfrak{t}(n)\right)^{\infty}_{n=1}=\left(\sum\limits^{m}_{i=1}v_{(n,i,1)}\ldots v_{(n,i,n)}\right)^{\infty}_{n=1},

where
v_{(n,k,1)}\ldots v_{(n,k,n)}
denotes the class of
v_{(n,k,1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{(n,k,n)}\in V^{\otimes n}
under the action of the symmetric group. If
X
is basis of
V
, then
\mathsf{P}(V)\cong\mathbb{F}\llbracket X\rrbracket_{+}

[
22
, Section 1.4.4]
, where
\mathbb{F}\llbracket X\rrbracket_{+}
is the non-unital associative ring of reduced power series over
X
, that is, power series over
X
with no constant/degree
0
term. Therefore,
\mathsf{P}(V)
is a non-unital associative
\mathbb{F}
-algebra. The algebra structure is induced by concatenation of classes of tensors:

*:v_{1}\dots v_{n}\otimes w_{1}\dots w_{k}\mapsto v_{1}\dots v_{n}w_{1}\dots w_{k}

which provides a commutative, associative multiplication
*:(V^{\otimes n})_{S(n)}\otimes(V^{\otimes k})_{S(k)}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(V^{\otimes n+k})_{S(n+k)}
. We will sometimes abbreviate a juxtaposition of a finite family
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
of elements of
V
(resp. a concatenation of a finite family of symmetrized tensors
(\underline{v}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
with
\underline{v}_{i}\in(V^{\otimes n_{i}})_{S(n_{i})}
) by:

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I}v_{i}=v_{1}\ldots v_{i},

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{resp. }\prod_{i\in I}\underline{v}_{i}=\underline{v}_{1}*\ldots*\underline{v}_{n}\right).

It is worth pointing out that
\mathsf{P}(V)
does not have a unit element. More specifically,
\mathsf{P}(V)
will not come equipped with a natural map of type
\mathbb{F}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{P}(V)
. So
\mathsf{P}(V)
will not induce an algebra modality, and therefore will not induce a differential category structure on
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}
.

The monad structure of
\mathsf{P}
corresponds to the composition of power series, which is closely related to the composition of polynomials. Define the functor
\mathsf{P}:\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}
as mapping an
\mathbb{F}
-vector space
V
to
\mathsf{P}(V)
, as defined above, and mapping an
\mathbb{F}
-linear map
f:V\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}W
to the
\mathbb{F}
-linear map
{\mathsf{P}(f):\mathsf{P}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{P}(W)}
defined on elements
\mathfrak{t}
as above by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}(f)(\mathfrak{t})=\left(\sum\limits^{m}_{i=1}f(v_{(n,i,1)})\ldots f(v_{(n,i,n)})\right)^{\infty}_{n=1}.

Define the monad unit
\eta_{V}:V\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{P}(V)
by:

\displaystyle\eta_{V}(v)=(v,0,0,\ldots).

From a power series point of view, if
X
is a basis of
V
,
\eta_{V}
maps a basis element
x\in X
to its associated monomial of degree
1
. For the monad multiplication, let us first consider an element
\mathfrak{s}\in\mathsf{P}\mathsf{P}(V)
, which is a list of symmetrized tensor products of lists of symmetrized tensor products,
\mathfrak{s}=\left(\mathfrak{s}(n)\right)^{\infty}_{n=1}
,
\mathfrak{s}(n)\in\left((\mathsf{P}(V))^{\otimes n}\right)_{S(n)}
and thus,
\mathfrak{s}(n)
is of the form:

\mathfrak{s}(n)=\sum\limits^{m}_{i=1}\mathfrak{s}(n)_{(i,1)}\ldots\mathfrak{s}(n)_{(i,n)}

for some
\mathfrak{s}(n)_{(i,j)}\in\mathsf{P}(V)
. Now for every partition of
n
not involving
0
, that is, for every
n_{1}+\ldots+n_{k}=n
with
n_{j}\geq 1
, define
\mathfrak{s}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})\in(V^{\otimes n})_{S(n)}
as follows:

\mathfrak{s}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})=\sum\limits^{m}_{i=1}\mathfrak{s}(k)_{(i,1)}(n_{1})*\ldots*\mathfrak{s}(k)_{(i,k)}(n_{k}),\

where
*
is the concatenation multiplication defined above. Define the monad multiplication
\mu_{V}:\mathsf{P}\mathsf{P}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{P}(V)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mu_{V}(\mathfrak{s})=\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{n_{1}+\ldots+n_{k}=n}\mathfrak{s}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})\right)^{\infty}_{n=1}

\displaystyle\mu_{V}(\mathfrak{s})(n)=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{n_{1}+\ldots+n_{k}=n}\mathfrak{s}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})

This monad multiplication corresponds to the composition of multivariable reduced power series, as defined explicitly in
[
7
, Section 4.1]
.

Lemma 5.1

[
22
, Section 1.4.3]

(\mathsf{P},\mu,\eta)
is a monad on
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}
.

We now introduce the differential combinator transformation for
\mathsf{P}
, which will correspond to differentiating power series.
Define
\partial_{V}:\mathsf{P}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{P}(V\times V)
by setting:

\displaystyle\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t})(n)=\sum\limits^{m}_{i=1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left((v_{(n,i,1)},0)\ldots\widehat{(v_{(n,i,j)},0)}\ldots(v_{(n,i,n)},0)\right)(0,v_{n,i,j}),

where
\mathfrak{t}
is an arbitrary element of
\mathsf{P}(V)
as above and
\widehat{(v_{(n,i,j)},0)}
indicates the omission of the factor
(v_{(n,i,j)},0)
in the product. With our conventions, we can abbreviate:

\displaystyle\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t})(n)=\sum\limits^{m}_{i=1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{k\neq j}(v_{(n,i,k)},0)\right)(0,v_{n,i,j}).

If
X
is a basis of
V
, the differential combinator transformation can described as a map
{\partial_{V}:\mathbb{F}\llbracket X\rrbracket_{+}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{F}\llbracket X\sqcup X\rrbracket_{+}}
which maps a reduced power series
\mathfrak{t}(\vec{x})
to its sum of its partial derivatives:

\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t}(\vec{x}))=\sum\limits_{x_{i}\in\vec{x}}\frac{\partial\mathfrak{t}(\vec{x})}{\partial x_{i}}x^{\ast}_{i}

where
x^{\ast}_{i}
denotes the element
x_{i}
in the second copy of
X
in the disjoint union
X\sqcup X
. Note that even if
\mathfrak{t}(\vec{x})
depends on an infinite list of variables,
\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t}(\vec{x}))
is well-defined as a formal power series.

It is worth insisting on the fact that
\partial
cannot be induced by a deriving transformation in the sense of Example
3.13
. Indeed, as a map,
\partial
does not factor through a map
\mathsf{P}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{P}(V)\otimes V
. Note that a power series could have infinite partial derivatives and, since infinite sums and
\otimes
are generally incompatible, the derivative of a power series could not be described as an element of
\mathsf{P}(V)\otimes V
. Moreover, we already noted the lack of unit: a differential operator of type
\mathsf{P}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{P}(V)\otimes V
would not be able to properly derive degree 1 monomials without a unit argument to put in the
\mathsf{P}(V)
component.

Proposition 5.2

\partial
is a differential combinator transformation on
(\mathsf{P},\mu,\eta)
.

Proof:

First, it is straightforward to see that
\partial
is indeed a natural transformation.
We must show that
\partial
satisfies the dual of the six axioms
[dc.1]
to
[dc.6]
from Definition
3.1
. Throughout this proof, it is sufficient to do the calculations on an arbitrary element
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathsf{P}(V)
. We can further assume that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathfrak{t}(n)=v_{(n,1)}*\ldots*v_{(n,n)}
(the integer
m
as above is equal to 1), and then extend by linearity.

[dc.1]

One has, for all
n>0
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}(\pi_{0})\circ\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t})(n)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{P}(\pi_{0})\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{k\neq j}(v_{(n,k)},0)\right)(0,v_{(n,j)})\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{k\neq j}v_{(n,k)}\right)0=0.

So
\mathsf{P}(\pi_{0})\circ\partial_{V}=0
.

[dc.2]

Let
\Delta:V\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}V\times V
be the diagonal map. One computes that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}(V\times\Delta)\circ\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t})(n)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{P}(V\times\Delta)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{k\neq j}(v_{(n,k)},0)\right)(0,v_{(n,j)})\right),

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{k\neq j}(v_{n,k},0,0)\right)(0,v_{(n,j)},v_{(n,j)}),

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{k\neq j}(v_{n,k},0,0)\right)(0,v_{(n,j)},0)\right)+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{k\neq j}(v_{n,k},0,0)\right)(0,0,v_{(n,j)})\right),

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{P}\mathsf{P}(V\times\iota_{0})+\mathsf{P}(V\times\iota_{1})\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{k\neq j}(v_{(n,k)},0)\right)(0,v_{(n,j)})\right),

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{P}(V\times\iota_{0})+\mathsf{P}(V\times\iota_{1})\right)\circ\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t})(n).

So
\mathsf{P}(1_{V}\times\Delta_{V})\circ\partial_{V}=\left(\mathsf{P}(1_{V}\times\iota_{0})+\mathsf{P}(1_{V}\times\iota_{1})\right)\circ\partial_{V}
.

[dc.3]

This is a straightforward verification. So
\partial_{V}\circ\eta_{V}=\eta_{V\times V}\circ\iota_{1}
.

[dc.4]

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be an element in
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{P}(V))
such that

\mathfrak{s}(k)=\mathfrak{s}(k)_{1}\dots\mathfrak{s}(k)_{k},

with, for all
j\in\{1,\dots,k\},n>0
,

\mathfrak{s}(k)_{j}(n)=v_{(k,j,n,1)}\dots v_{(k,j,n,n)},

where
v_{(k,j,n,l)}\in V
for all
k,j,n,l
. Note that elements of this type span
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{P}(V))
. We then can make the computation on
\mathfrak{s}
and extend by linearity.

On one hand, for all
n>0
,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathfrak{s})(n)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{n_{1}+\ldots+n_{k}=n}\mathfrak{s}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}),

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{n_{1}+\ldots+n_{k}=n}\mathfrak{s}(k)_{1}(n_{1})*\ldots*\mathfrak{s}(k)_{k}(n_{k}),

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{n_{1}+\ldots+n_{k}=n}v_{(k,1,n_{1},1)}\ldots v_{(k,1,n_{1},n_{1})}\ldots v_{(k,k,n_{k},1)}\ldots v_{(k,k,n_{k},n_{k})},

and so,

\partial_{V}\circ\mu_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathfrak{s})(n)=\\
\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{n_{1}+\ldots+n_{k}=n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{l=1}^{n_{j}}(v_{(k,1,n_{1},1)},0)\ldots(v_{(k,1,n_{1},n_{1})},0)\ldots\widehat{(v_{(k,j,n_{j},l)},0)}\ldots(v_{(k,k,n_{k},n_{k})},0)\ (0,v_{(k,j,n_{j},l)})

On the other hand, for all
k>0
,

\partial_{\mathsf{P}(V)}(\mathfrak{s})(k)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathfrak{(}s(k)_{1},0)\ldots\widehat{(s(k)_{j},0)}\ldots(s(k)_{k},0)\ (0,s(k)_{j}),

So if
[-,-]
is the pairing operator for the coproduct, we have that:

\mathsf{P}\left(\left[\mathsf{P}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}\right]\right)\circ\partial_{\mathsf{P}(V)}(\mathfrak{s})(k)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathsf{P}(\iota_{0})(\mathfrak{s}(k)_{1})\ldots\widehat{\mathsf{P}(\iota_{0})(\mathfrak{s}(k)_{j})}\ldots\mathsf{P}(\iota_{0})(\mathfrak{s}(k)_{k})\ \partial_{V}(\mathfrak{s}(k)_{j}),

and so, for all
n>0
,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathsf{P}(V)}\circ\mathsf{P}\left(\left[\mathsf{P}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}\right]\right)\circ\partial_{\mathsf{P}(V)}(\mathfrak{s})(n)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=n}\mathsf{P}\left(\left[\mathsf{P}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}\right]\right)\circ\partial_{\mathsf{P}(V)}(\mathfrak{s})(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathsf{P}(\iota_{0})(\mathfrak{s}(k)_{1})*\ldots*\widehat{\mathsf{P}(\iota_{0})(\mathfrak{s}(k)_{j})}*\ldots*\mathsf{P}(\iota_{0})(\mathfrak{s}(k)_{k})\ *\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{s}(k)_{j}),

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{n_{1}+\ldots+n_{k}=n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{l=1}^{n_{j}}(v_{(k,1,n_{1},1)},0)\ldots(v_{(k,1,n_{1},n_{1})},0)\ldots\widehat{(v_{(k,j,n_{j},l)},0)}\ldots(v_{(k,k,n_{k},n_{k})},0)\ (0,v_{(k,j,n_{j},l)})

So
\partial_{V}\circ\mu_{V}=\mu_{V\times V}\circ\mathsf{P}\left([\mathsf{P}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}]\right)\circ\partial_{\mathsf{P}(V)}
.

In order to prove the two last identities, let us compute
\partial_{V\times V}(\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t}))
. For all
n>0
,

\displaystyle\partial_{V\times V}(\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t}))(n)=

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{0}\neq j_{1}\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\left(\prod_{k\neq j_{0},j_{1}}(v_{(n,i,k)},0,0,0)\right)(0,v_{n,i,j_{0}},0,0)(0,0,v_{n,i,j_{1}},0)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{k\neq j}(v_{(n,i,k)},0,0,0)\right)(0,0,0,v_{n,i,j})

[dc.5]

Using our computation for
\partial_{V\times V}(\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t}))
, we get, for all
n>0
:

\mathsf{P}(\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})\circ\partial_{V\times V}(\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t}))(n)=\\
\sum_{j_{0}\neq j_{1}\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\left(\prod_{k\neq j_{0},j_{1}}(v_{(n,i,k)},0)\right)(0,0)(0,0)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{k\neq j}(v_{(n,i,k)},0)\right)(0,v_{n,i,j})=\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t})

so
\mathsf{P}(\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})\circ\partial_{V\times V}(\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t}))=\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t})
.

[dc.6]

Our computation of
\partial_{V\times V}(\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{t}))
clearly shows that this element is fixed under the action of
\mathsf{P}(c_{V})
. So
\mathsf{P}(c_{V})\circ\partial_{V\times V}\circ\partial_{V}=\partial_{V\times V}\circ\partial_{V}
.

So we conclude that
\partial
is a differential combinator transformation.
\Box

Thus
(\mathsf{P},\mu,\eta,\partial)
is a coCartesian differential monad and so the opposite of its Kleisli category is a Cartesian differential category (which we summarize in Corollary
5.4
below) which, as we will explain below, captures power series differentiation. This coCartesian differential monad also comes equipped with a
\mathsf{D}
-linear counit. Define
\varepsilon_{V}:\mathsf{P}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}V
as simply the projection onto
V
:

\varepsilon_{V}\left(\mathfrak{t}\right)=\mathfrak{t}(1)

From a power series point of view,
\varepsilon
projects out the degree 1 coefficients of a reduced power series.

Lemma 5.3

\varepsilon
is a
\mathsf{D}
-linear counit of
(\mathsf{P},\mu,\eta,\partial)
.

Proof:
The proof, which is a straightforward verification, is left to the reader as an exercise.

\Box

Therefore, the subcategory of
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps of the opposite category of the Kleisli category of
\mathsf{P}
is isomorphic to the opposite category of
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}
. We summarize these results as follows:

Corollary 5.4

(\mathsf{P},\mu,\eta,\partial)
is a Cartesian differential comonad on
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}
with
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit
\varepsilon
. Therefore
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{P}}
is a Cartesian differential category and
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{P}}\right]\cong\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}
.

The Cartesian differential category
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{P}}
can be interpreted as the category whose objects are
\mathbb{F}
-vector spaces and whose maps are reduced power series between them. As a result, focusing on the finite-dimensional vector spaces, specifically
\mathbb{F}^{n}
, one obtains a Cartesian differential category of reduced power series over finite variables. We describe this category in detail.

Example 5.5

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a field. Define the category
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{POW}_{red}
whose object are
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where a map
{\mathfrak{P}:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
is a
m
-tuple of reduced power series (i.e. power series with no degree
0
coefficients) in
n
variables, that is,
\mathfrak{P}=\langle\mathfrak{p}_{1}(\vec{x}),\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{m}(\vec{x})\rangle
with
\mathfrak{p}_{i}(\vec{x})\in\mathbb{F}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rrbracket_{+}
. The identity maps
1_{n}:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n
are the tuples
1_{n}=\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
and where composition is given by multivariable power series substitution
[
7
, Section 4.1]
.
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{POW}_{red}
is a Cartesian left additive category where the finite product structure is given by
n\times m=n+m
with projection maps
{\pi_{0}:n\times m\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n}
and
{\pi_{1}:n\times m\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
defined as the tuples
\pi_{0}=\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
and
\pi_{1}=\langle x_{n+1},\ldots,x_{n+m}\rangle
, and where the additive structure is defined coordinate-wise via the standard sum of power series.
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{POW}_{red}
is also a Cartesian differential category where the differential combinator is given by the standard differentiation of power series, that is, for a map
{\mathfrak{P}:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
, with
\mathfrak{P}=\langle\mathfrak{p}_{1}(\vec{x}),\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{m}(\vec{x})\rangle
, its derivative
\mathsf{D}[\mathfrak{P}]:n\times n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m
is defined as the tuple of the sum of the partial derivatives of the power series
\mathfrak{p}_{i}(\vec{x})
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}[\mathfrak{P}](\vec{x},\vec{y}):=\left(\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial\mathfrak{p}_{1}(\vec{x})}{\partial x_{i}}y_{i},\ldots,\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial\mathfrak{p}_{n}(\vec{x})}{\partial x_{i}}y_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial\mathfrak{p}_{j}(\vec{x})}{\partial x_{i}}y_{i}\in\mathbb{F}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\rrbracket_{+}

It is important to note that even if
\mathfrak{p}_{j}(\vec{x})
has terms of degree 1, every partial derivative
\frac{\partial\mathfrak{p}_{j}(\vec{x})}{\partial x_{i}}y_{i}
will still be reduced (even if
\frac{\partial\mathfrak{p}_{j}(\vec{x})}{\partial x_{i}}
has a degree 0 term), and thus the differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
is indeed well-defined. A map
{\mathfrak{P}:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if it of the form:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}=\left\langle\sum\limits^{n}_{i=0}r_{i,1}x_{i},\ldots,\sum\limits^{n}_{i=0}r_{i,m}x_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle r_{i,j}\in\mathbb{F}

Thus
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{POW}_{red}]
is equivalent to
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{LIN}
(as defined in Example
2.7
). We note that this example generalize to the category of reduced formal power over an arbitrary commutative (semi)ring.

Observe that we also have the following chain of isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{POW}_{red}(n,1)=\mathbb{F}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rrbracket_{+}\cong\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{F}^{n})\cong\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}\left(\mathbb{F},\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{F}^{n})\right)=\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}_{\mathsf{P}}\left(\mathbb{F},\mathbb{F}^{n}\right)=\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{P}}\left(\mathbb{F}^{n},\mathbb{F}\right)

which then implies that
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{POW}_{red}(n,m)\cong\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{P}}\left(\mathbb{F}^{n},\mathbb{F}^{m}\right)
. Thus
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{POW}_{red}
is isomorphic to the full subcategory of
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{P}}
whose objects are the finite dimensional
\mathbb{F}
-vector spaces. In the finite dimensional case, the differential combinator transformation corresponds precisely to the differential combinator on
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{POW}_{red}
:

\partial_{\mathbb{F}^{n}}(\mathfrak{p}(\vec{x}))=\mathsf{D}[\mathfrak{p}](\vec{x},\vec{y})

Therefore,
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{POW}_{red}
is a sub-Cartesian differential category of
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{P}}
, where the latter allows for power series over infinite variables.

6
Example: Divided Power Algebras

In this section, we show that the free divided power algebra monad is a coCartesian differential monad, and therefore, we obtain a Cartesian differential category of divided power polynomials
[
30
, Section 12]
. Divided power algebras were introduced by Cartan
[
8
]
to study the homology of Eilenberg-MacLane spaces with coefficients in a prime field of positive characteristic. Such structures appear notably on the homotopy of simplicial algebras
[
8
,
23
]
, and in the study of
D
-modules and crystalline cohomology
[
2
]
. The free divided power algebra monad
\mathsf{\Gamma}
was first introduced by Roby in
[
29
]
and generalized in the context of operads by Fresse in
[
23
]
. Much as for reduced power series, the composition of divided power polynomials is only well-defined when they are reduced, that is, have no constant term. More generally, the study of divided power algebras has been widely developed in the non-unital setting
[
23
]
. Since the monad we study encodes a structure of non-unital algebras, this provides another example of a Cartesian differential comonad which is not induced by a differential category. We begin by reviewing the definition of a divided power algebra.

Definition 6.1

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a field.

A divided power algebra

[
8
, Section 2]
over
\mathbb{F}
is a commutative associative (non-unital)
\mathbb{F}
-algebra
(A,*)
, where
A
is the underlying
\mathbb{F}
-vector space and
\ast
is the
\mathbb{F}
-bilinear multiplication, which comes equipped with a divided power structure, that is, a family of functions
(-)^{[n]}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
,
a\mapsto a^{[n]}
, indexed by strictly positive integers
n
, such that the following identities hold:

[dp.1]

(\lambda a)^{[n]}=\lambda^{n}a^{[n]}
for all
a\in A
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}
.

[dp.2]

a^{[m]}*a^{[n]}=\binom{m+n}{m}a^{[m+n]}
for all
a\in A
.

[dp.3]

(a+b)^{[n]}=a^{[n]}+\big(\sum_{l=1}^{n-1}a^{[l]}*b^{[n-l]}\big)+b^{[n]}
for all
a\in A
,
b\in A
.

[dp.4]

a^{[1]}=a
for all
a\in A
.

[dp.5]

(a*b)^{[n]}=n!a^{[n]}*b^{[n]}=a^{*n}*b^{[n]}=a^{[n]}*b^{*n}
for all
a\in A
,
b\in A
.

[dp.6]

(a^{[n]})^{[m]}=\frac{(mn)!}{m!(n!)^{m}}a^{[mn]}
for all
a\in A
.

The function
(-)^{[n]}
is called the
n
-th divided power operation.

When the base field
\mathbb{F}
is of characteristic
0
, the only divided power structure on a commutative associative algebra
(A,*)
is given by
a^{[n]}=\frac{a^{*n}}{n!}
, which justifies the name “divided powers”. Therefore, in the characteristic
0
case, a divided power algebra is simply a commutative associative (non-unital) algebra. However, in general, for non-zero characteristics, the two notions diverge. Examples of divided power algebras include the homology of Eilenberg-MacLane spaces
[
8
, Section 5 and 8]
, the homotopy of simplicial commutative algebras
[
8
, Théorème 1]
, and all Zinbiel algebras (which we review in the next section)
[
18
, Theorem 3.4]
. Furthermore, there exists a notion of free divided power algebras, which we review now.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a field. For an
\mathbb{F}
-vector space
V
, define
\mathsf{\Gamma}_{n}(V)=(V^{\otimes n})^{S(n)}\subseteq V^{\otimes n}
as the subspace of tensors of length
n
of
V
which are fixed under the action of the symmetric group
S(n)
, that is, invariant under all
n
-permutations
\sigma\in S(n)
. Categorically speaking,
\mathsf{\Gamma}_{n}(V)
is the joint equalizer of the
n
-permutations. Define
\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)
as follows:

\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)=\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{n}(V)=V\oplus\mathsf{\Gamma}_{2}(V)\oplus\mathsf{\Gamma}_{3}(V)\oplus\ldots

The vector space
\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)
is endowed with a divided power algebra structure, and is the free divided power algebra over
V

[
8
, Section 2]
. Explicitly, the divided power operations and the product are defined on generators
v,w\in V
by:

v^{[n]}=v^{\otimes n}\hskip 18.49988ptv*w=v\otimes w+w\otimes v.

An arbitrary element of
\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)
can then be expressed as a finite sum of divided power monomials
[
9
, Section 4]
, which are elements of the form:

v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]}

for
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V
, where
\ast
is the multiplication of
\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)
, and
(-)^{[r_{j}]}
are the divided power operations. Note that this decomposition in monomials is not unique in general. Later on, we will define the differential combinator on monomials. In order to check that this combinator is well defined, one can use the explicit form of such a monomial:

v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]}=\sum_{\sigma\in S(n)/S(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})}\sigma(v_{1}^{\otimes r_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n}^{\otimes r_{n}}),

where
S(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})=S(r_{1})\times\ldots\times S(r_{p})
is the Young subgroup of the symmetric group
S(r_{1}+\ldots+r_{p})
.

Free divided power algebras induce a monad
\mathsf{\Gamma}
on
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}
. Note that it is sufficient to define the monad structure maps on divided power monomials and then extend by linearity. Define the endofunctor
\mathsf{\Gamma}:\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}
which sends a
\mathbb{F}
-vector space
V
to its free divided power algebra
\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)
, and which sends an
\mathsf{F}
-linear map
f:V\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}W
to the
\mathsf{F}
-linear map
\mathsf{\Gamma}(f):\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{\Gamma}(W)
defined on divided powers monomials as follows:

\mathsf{\Gamma}(f)(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{p}^{[r_{n}]})=(f(v_{1}))^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(f(v_{n}))^{[r_{n}]}

which we then extend by linearity. The monad unit
\eta_{V}:V\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)
is the injection map of
V
into
\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)
:

\eta_{V}(v)=v^{[1]}.

Note that, with this notation, the zero element of
\Gamma(V)
will here be denoted by
0^{[1]}
. The monad multiplication
{\mu_{V}:\mathsf{\Gamma}(\mathsf{\Gamma}(V))\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)}
is defined as follows on divided power monomials of divided power monomials, using
[dp.5]
and
[dp.6]
:

\displaystyle\mu_{V}\left((v_{1,1}^{[q_{1,1}]}*\ldots*v_{1,k_{1}}^{[q_{1,k_{1}}]})^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{p,1}^{[q_{p,1}]}*\ldots*v_{p,k_{p}}^{[q_{p,k_{p}}]})^{[r_{p}]}\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\prod_{i=1}^{p}\frac{1}{r_{i}!}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{i}}\frac{(r_{i}q_{i,j})!}{q_{i,j}!^{r_{i}}}\right)v_{1,1}^{[r_{1}q_{1,1}]}*\ldots*v_{1,k_{1}}^{[r_{1}q_{1,k_{1}}]}*\ldots*v_{p,k_{p}}^{[r_{p}q_{p,k_{p}}]}

which we then extend by linearity.
Note that the functor
\mathsf{\Gamma}
, and the monad structure we described, can be constructed from the operad of commutative (non-unital) algebras
[
23
, Proposition 1.2.3]
. Furthermore, note that the algebras of the monad
\mathsf{\Gamma}
are precisely the dividied power algebras
[
29
, Section 10, Théorème 1 and 2]
.

Lemma 6.2

[
23
, Proposition 1.2.3]

(\mathsf{\Gamma},\mu,\eta)
is a monad.

Observe that
\mathsf{\Gamma}
will not be an algebra modality since
\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)
is non-unital. Therefore,
\mathsf{\Gamma}
will provide an example of Cartesian differential comonad which is not induced from a differential category structure. We now define the differential combinator transformation for
\mathsf{\Gamma}
. Define
\partial_{V}:\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{\Gamma}(V\times V)
as follows on divided power monomials:

\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,v_{i})^{[1]}

which we then extend by linearity. If
r_{i}=1
, we use the following convention:

(v_{1},0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,v_{i})^{[1]}\!=\!(v_{1},0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i-1},0)^{[r_{i-1}]}*(v_{i+1},0)^{[r_{i+1}]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,v_{i})^{[1]}

We will see below that
\partial
corresponds to taking the sum of the partial derivatives of divided power polynomials. Note that a consequence of the lack of a unit in
\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)
is that
\partial_{V}
does not factor through a map
\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)\otimes V
since such a map would be undefined on the divided power monomials of degree 1,
v^{[1]}
.

Proposition 6.3

\partial
is a differential combinator transformation for
(\mathsf{\Gamma},\mu,\eta)
.

Proof:
Throughout this proof, it is sufficient to do the calculations on divided power monomials and then extend by linearity. First, clearly
\partial
is a natural transformation. We now need to show that
\partial
satisfies the dual of the six axioms
[dc.1]
to
[dc.6]
from Definition
3.1
.

[dc.1]

Here we use the fact that multiplication by zero gives zero:

\displaystyle\mathsf{\Gamma}(\pi_{0})\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{\Gamma}(\pi_{0})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,v_{i})^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\pi_{0}(v_{1},0)\right)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*\left(\pi_{0}(v_{i},0)\right)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*\left(\pi_{0}(v_{n},0)\right)^{[r_{n}]}*\left(\pi_{0}(0,v_{i})\right)^{[1]}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{i}^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]}*0^{[1]}

\displaystyle=~0^{[1]}

so
\mathsf{\Gamma}(\pi_{0})\circ\partial_{V}=0

[dc.2]

Here we use the fact that, by
[dp.3]
,
(v+w)^{[1]}=v^{[1]}+w^{[1]}
, and the multilinearity of the multiplication:

\displaystyle\mathsf{\Gamma}(1_{V}\times\Delta_{V})\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})\right)=\mathsf{\Gamma}(1_{V}\times\Delta_{V})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,v_{i})^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},\Delta_{V}(0))^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},\Delta_{V}(0))^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},\Delta_{V}(0))^{[r_{n}]}*(0,\Delta_{V}(v_{i}))^{[1]}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0,0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0,0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0,0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,v_{i},v_{i})^{[1]}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0,0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0,0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0,0)^{[r_{n}]}*\left((0,v_{i},0)+(0,0,v_{i})\right)^{[1]}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0,0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0,0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0,0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,v_{i},0)^{[1]}

\displaystyle+~\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0,0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0,0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0,0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,0,v_{i})^{[1]}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},\iota_{0}(0))^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},\iota_{0}(0))^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},\iota_{0}(0))^{[r_{n}]}*(0,\iota_{0}(v_{i}))^{[1]}

\displaystyle+~\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},\iota_{1}(0))^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},\iota_{1}(0))^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},\iota_{1}(0))^{[r_{n}]}*(0,\iota_{1}(v_{i}))^{[1]}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{\Gamma}(1_{V}\times\iota_{0})\left((v_{1},0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,v_{i})^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle+~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{\Gamma}(1_{V}\times\iota_{1})\left((v_{1},0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,v_{i})^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{\Gamma}(1_{V}\times\iota_{0})\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})\right)+\mathsf{\Gamma}(1_{V}\times\iota_{1})\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathsf{\Gamma}(1_{V}\times\iota_{0})+\mathsf{\Gamma}(1_{V}\times\iota_{1})\right)\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})\right)

So
\Gamma(V\times\Delta_{V})\circ\partial_{V}=\left(\mathsf{\Gamma}(1_{V}\times\iota_{0})+\mathsf{\Gamma}(1_{V}\times\iota_{1})\right)\circ\partial_{V}
.

[dc.3]

This is a straightforward. So
\partial_{V}\circ\eta_{V}=\eta_{V\times V}\circ\iota_{1}
.

[dc.4]

Carefully using the divided power structure axiom
[dp.2]
when expanding out divided power monomials of divided power monomials, we compute:

\displaystyle\mu_{V\times V}\left(\mathsf{\Gamma}\left(\left[\mathsf{\Gamma}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}\right]\right)\left(\partial_{\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)}\left((v_{1,1}^{[q_{1,1}]}*\ldots*v_{1,k_{1}}^{[q_{1,k_{1}}]})^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{p,1}^{[q_{p,1}]}*\ldots*v_{p,k_{p}}^{[q_{p,k_{p}}]})^{[r_{p}]}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\mu_{V\times V}\left(\mathsf{\Gamma}\left[\mathsf{\Gamma}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}\right]\left(\sum^{p}_{i=1}\left((v_{1,1}^{[q_{1,1}]}*\ldots*v_{1,k_{1}}^{[q_{1,k_{1}}]}),0\right)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*\left((v_{i,1}^{[q_{i,1}]}*\ldots*v_{i,k_{i}}^{[q_{i,k_{i}}]}),0\right)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots\right.\right.

\displaystyle~\left.\left.*\left((v_{p,1}^{[q_{p,1}]}*\ldots*v_{p,k_{p}}^{[q_{p,k_{p}}]}),0\right)^{[r_{p}]}*\left(0,(v_{i,1}^{[q_{i,1}]}*\ldots*v_{i,k_{i}}^{[q_{i,k_{i}}]})\right)^{[1]}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\mu_{V\times V}\left(\sum^{p}_{i=1}\left(\iota_{0}(v_{1,1})^{[q_{1,1}]}*\ldots*\iota_{0}(v_{1,k_{1}})^{[q_{1,k_{1}}]}\right)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*\left(\iota_{0}(v_{i,1})^{[q_{i,1}]}*\ldots*\iota_{0}(v_{i,k_{i}})^{[q_{i,k_{i}}]}\right)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots\right.

\displaystyle~\left.*\left(\iota_{0}(v_{p,1})^{[q_{p,1}]}*\ldots*\iota_{0}(v_{p,k_{p}})^{[q_{p,k_{p}}]}\right)^{[r_{p}]}*\left(\partial_{V}\left(v_{i,1}^{[q_{i,1}]}*\ldots*v_{i,k_{i}}^{[q_{i,k_{i}}]}\right)\right)^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=~\mu_{V\times V}\left(\sum^{p}_{i=1}\left((v_{1,1},0)^{[q_{1,1}]}*\ldots*(v_{1,k_{1}},0)^{[q_{1,k_{1}}]}\right)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*\left((v_{i,1},0)^{[q_{i,1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i,k_{i}},0)^{[q_{i,k_{i}}]}\right)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots\right.

\displaystyle~\left.*\left((v_{p,1},0)^{[q_{p,1}]}*\ldots*(v_{p,k_{p}},0)^{[q_{p,k_{p}}]}\right)^{[r_{p}]}*\right.

\displaystyle~\left.\left(\sum^{k_{i}}_{j=1}(v_{i,1},0)^{[q_{i,1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i,j},0)^{[q_{i,j}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{i,k_{i}},0)^{[q_{i,k_{i}}]}*(0,v_{i,j})^{[1]}\right)^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=~\mu_{V\times V}\left(\sum^{p}_{i=1}\sum^{k_{i}}_{j=1}\left((v_{1,1},0)^{[q_{1,1}]}*\ldots*(v_{1,k_{1}},0)^{[q_{1,k_{1}}]}\right)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*\left((v_{i,1},0)^{[q_{i,1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i,k_{i}},0)^{[q_{i,k_{i}}]}\right)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots\right.

\displaystyle~\left.*\left((v_{p,1},0)^{[q_{p,1}]}*\ldots*(v_{p,k_{p}},0)^{[q_{p,k_{p}}]}\right)^{[r_{p}]}*\left((v_{i,1},0)^{[q_{i,1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i,j},0)^{[q_{i,j}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{i,k_{i}},0)^{[q_{i,k_{i}}]}*(0,v_{i,j})^{[1]}\right)^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=~\sum^{p}_{i=1}\sum^{k_{i}}_{j=1}\left(\prod_{i_{0}=1}^{p}\frac{1}{r_{i_{0}}!}\prod_{j_{0}=1}^{k_{i_{0}}}\frac{(r_{i_{0}}q_{i_{0},j_{0}})!}{q_{i_{0},j_{0}}!^{r_{i_{0}}}}\right)(v_{1,1},0)^{[r_{1}q_{1,1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i,j},0)^{[r_{i}q_{i,j}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{p,k_{p}},0)^{[r_{p}q_{p,k_{p}}]}*(0,v_{i,j})^{[1]}

\displaystyle=~\partial_{V}\left(\left(\prod_{i_{0}=1}^{p}\frac{1}{r_{i_{0}}!}\prod_{j_{0}=1}^{k_{i_{0}}}\frac{(r_{i_{0}}q_{i_{0},j_{0}})!}{q_{i_{0},j_{0}}!^{r_{i_{0}}}}\right)v_{1,1}^{[r_{1}q_{1,1}]}*\ldots*v_{1,k_{1}}^{[r_{1}q_{1,k_{1}}]}*\ldots*v_{p,k_{p}}^{[r_{p}q_{p,k_{p}}]}\right)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{V}\left(\mu_{V}\left((v_{1,1}^{[q_{1,1}]}*\ldots*v_{1,k_{1}}^{[q_{1,k_{1}}]})^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{p,1}^{[q_{p,1}]}*\ldots*v_{p,k_{p}}^{[q_{p,k_{p}}]})^{[r_{p}]}\right)\right)

So
\partial_{V}\circ\mu_{V}=\mu_{V\times V}\circ\mathsf{\Gamma}\left[\mathsf{\Gamma}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}\right]\circ\partial_{\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)}

For the last two axioms, it will be useful to first compute
\partial_{V\times V}\circ\partial_{V}
separately. So we compute:

\displaystyle\partial_{V\times V}\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})\right)=~\partial_{V\times V}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,v_{i})^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(v_{1},0,0,0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{j},0,0,0)^{[r_{j}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0,0,0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0,0,0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,v_{i},0,0)^{[1]}*(0,0,v_{j},0)^{[1]}

\displaystyle+~\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0,0,0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0,0,0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0,0,0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,0,0,v_{i})^{[1]}

Thus we have that:

\displaystyle\partial_{V\times V}\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})\right)
