\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod\limits^{n}_{k=1}(v_{k},0,0,0)^{[r_{k}-\delta_{k,i}-\delta_{k,j}]}\right)*(0,v_{i},0,0)^{[1]}*(0,0,v_{j},0)^{[1]}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0,0,0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0,0,0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0,0,0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,0,0,v_{i})^{[1]}

where
\delta_{x,y}=0
if
x\neq y
and
\delta_{x,y}=1
if
x=y
, and we used
\prod
to denote a
*
-product of a family of elements. Note that there is a slight abuse of notation here in the case that
r_{i}=1
and when
i=j=i
, potentially giving
r_{i}-\delta_{i,i}-\delta_{j,j}=-1
. However, when
r_{i}=1
, the term
(v_{i},0)^{[r_{i}-1]}
vanishes, and so, this is not a problem.

[dc.5]

Here we use the fact that the first part of
\partial_{V\times V}\circ\partial_{V}
vanishes under
\mathsf{\Gamma}(\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})
(since we are multiplying by zero), while the second part becomes
\partial_{V}
under
\mathsf{\Gamma}(\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{\Gamma}(\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})\left(\partial_{V\times V}\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{\Gamma}(\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod\limits^{n}_{k=1}(v_{k},0,0,0)^{[r_{k}-\delta_{k,i}-\delta_{k,j}]}\right)*(0,v_{i},0,0)^{[1]}*(0,0,v_{j},0)^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle+~\mathsf{\Gamma}(\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0,0,0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0,0,0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0,0,0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,0,0,v_{i})^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod\limits^{n}_{k=1}\left(\pi_{0}(v_{k},0),\pi_{1}(0,0)\right)^{[r_{k}-\delta_{k,i}-\delta_{k,j}]}\right)*\left(\pi_{0}(0,v_{i})\pi_{1}(0,0)\right)^{[1]}*\left(\pi_{0}(0,0),\pi_{1}(v_{j},0)\right)^{[1]}

\displaystyle+~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\pi_{0}(v_{1},0),\pi_{1}(0,0)\right)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*\left(\pi_{0}(v_{i},0),\pi_{1}(0,0)\right)^{[r_{i}-1]}*

\displaystyle~~~~~~\ldots*\left(\pi_{0}(v_{n},0),\pi_{1}(0,0)\right)^{[r_{n}]}*\left(\pi_{0}(0,0),\pi_{1}(0,v_{i})\right)^{[1]}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod\limits^{n}_{k=1}\left(v_{k},0\right)^{[r_{k}-\delta_{k,i}-\delta_{k,j}]}\right)*\left(0,0\right)^{[1]}*\left(0,0\right)^{[1]}

\displaystyle+~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(v_{1},0\right)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*\left(v_{i},0\right)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*\left(v_{n},0\right)^{[r_{n}]}*\left(0,v_{i}\right)^{[1]}

\displaystyle=~0^{[1]}+\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})

\displaystyle=~\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})

So
\mathsf{\Gamma}(\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})\circ\partial_{V\times V}\circ\partial_{V}=\partial_{V}
.

[dc.6]

Here we use commutativity of the multiplication, which will essentially swap the order of the
i
and
j
index in the first part of
\partial_{V\times V}\circ\partial_{V}
, while the second part is unchanged:

\displaystyle\mathsf{\Gamma}(c_{V})\left(\partial_{V\times V}\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{\Gamma}(c_{V})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod\limits^{n}_{k=1}(v_{k},0,0,0)^{[r_{k}-\delta_{k,i}-\delta_{k,j}]}\right)*(0,v_{i},0,0)^{[1]}*(0,0,v_{j},0)^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle+~\mathsf{\Gamma}(c_{V})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0,0,0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0,0,0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0,0,0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,0,0,v_{i})^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod\limits^{n}_{k=1}\left(c_{V}(v_{k},0,0,0)\right)^{[r_{k}-\delta_{k,i}-\delta_{k,j}]}\right)*\left(c_{V}(0,v_{i},0,0)\right)^{[1]}*\left((\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})(0,0,v_{j},0)\right)^{[1]}

\displaystyle+~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(c_{V}(v_{1},0,0,0)\right)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*\left(c_{V}(v_{i},0,0,0)\right)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*\left(c_{V}(v_{n},0,0,0)\right)^{[r_{n}]}*\left(c_{V}(0,0,0,v_{i})\right)^{[1]}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\prod\limits^{n}_{k=1}(v_{k},0,0,0)^{[r_{k}-\delta_{k,i}-\delta_{k,j}]}\right)*(0,0,v_{i},0)^{[1]}*(0,v_{j},0,0)^{[1]}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v_{1},0,0,0)^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*(v_{i},0,0,0)^{[r_{i}-1]}*\ldots*(v_{n},0,0,0)^{[r_{n}]}*(0,0,0,v_{i})^{[1]}

\displaystyle=~\partial_{V\times V}\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})\right)

So
\mathsf{\Gamma}(c_{V})\circ\partial_{V\times V}\circ\partial_{V}=\partial_{V\times V}\circ\partial_{V}
.

So we conclude that
\partial
is a differential combinator transformation.
\Box

Thus,
(\mathsf{\Gamma},\mu,\eta,\partial)
is a coCartesian differential monad and so the opposite of its Kleisli category is a Cartesian differential category (which we summarize in Corollary
6.5
below) which, as we will explain below, captures differentiation of divided power polynomials. The coCartesian differential monad
(\mathsf{\Gamma},\mu,\eta,\partial)
also comes equipped with a
\mathsf{D}
-linear counit. Define
\varepsilon_{V}:\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}V
as follows on divided power monomials:

\varepsilon_{V}(v_{1}^{[r_{1}]}*\ldots*v_{n}^{[r_{n}]})=\begin{cases}v_{1},&\mbox{if }n=1,r_{n}=1,\\
0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

which we extend by linearity. Thus
\varepsilon_{V}
picks out the divided power monomials of degree 1,
v^{[1]}
for all
v\in V
, while mapping the rest to zero.

Lemma 6.4

\varepsilon
is a
\mathsf{D}
-linear counit of
(\mathsf{\Gamma},\mu,\eta,\partial)
.

Proof:
The proof is straightforward, and so we leave this as an excercise for the reader.

\Box

Thus the subcategory of
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps of the opposite category of the Kleisli category of
\mathsf{\Gamma}
is isomorphic to the opposite category of
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}
. Summarizing, we obtain the following statement:

Corollary 6.5

(\mathsf{\Gamma},\mu,\eta,\partial)
is a Cartesian differential comonad on
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}
with
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit
\varepsilon
. Therefore
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
is a Cartesian differential category and
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\right]\cong\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}
.

The Kleisli category
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
is closely related to the notion of (reduced) divided power polynomials. For a set
X
, we denote by
\mathbb{F}\lceil X\rceil
the ring of reduced divided power polynomials over the set
X
, which is by definition the free divided power algebra over the
\mathbb{F}
-vector space with basis
X

[
30
, Section 12]
. In other words, a reduced divided polynomial with variables in
X
is an
\mathbb{F}
-linear composition of commutative monomials of the type
x_{1}^{[k_{1}]}\ldots x_{n}^{[k_{n}]}
where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
is a tuple of
n
different elements of
X
and
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}
are strictly positive integers. By reduced, we mean that these polynomials do not have degree 0 terms. Multiplication is given by concatenation, multilinearity and the relation
[dp.2]
of Definition
6.1
. Composition of divided polynomials can be deduced from the relations
[dp.1]
,
[dp.3]
,
[dp.5]
and
[dp.6]
of
6.1
. For example, if
p(x)=x^{[n]}
, and
q(y,z)=y^{[m]}z
, then:

p(q(y,z))=(y^{[m]}z)^{[n]}=n!(y^{[m]})^{[n]}z^{[n]}=\frac{(mn)!}{(m!)^{n}}y^{[mn]}z^{[n]}.

We can define a notion of formal partial derivation for divided polynomials. For
x\in X
, define the linear map
{\frac{d}{dx}:\mathbb{F}\lceil X\rceil\!\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{F}\lceil X\rceil\oplus\mathbb{F}}
on monomials (which we then extend by linearity).
For all monomial
m=x_{1}^{[k_{1}]}\ldots x_{n}^{[k_{n}]}
,
\frac{d}{dx}(m)=0
if
x\neq x_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
\frac{d}{dx}(m)=x_{1}^{[k_{1}]}\ldots x_{j-1}^{[k_{j-1}]}x_{j}^{[k_{j}-1]}x_{j+1}^{[k_{j+1}]}\ldots x_{n}^{[k_{n}]}
if
x=x_{j}
and
k_{j}>1
,
\frac{d}{dx}(m)=x_{1}^{[k_{1}]}\ldots x_{j-1}^{[k_{j-1}]}x_{j+1}^{[k_{j+1}]}\ldots x_{n}^{[k_{n}]}
if
x=x_{j}
,
k_{j}=1
, and
n>1
, and finally,
\frac{d}{dx}(x)=1_{\mathbb{F}}
where
1_{\mathbb{F}}\in\mathbb{F}
is a generator of the second term of the direct sum
\mathbb{F}\lceil X\rceil\oplus\mathbb{F}
given by the unit of
\mathbb{F}
. We note that, in the case where
X
is a singleton, these definitions correspond to the notion of derivation for formal divided power series, also called Hurwitz series, as defined by Keigher and Pritchard in
[
26
]
. We can restrict to the finite dimensional case and obtain a sub-Cartesian differential category of
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
which is isomorphic to the Lawvere theory of reduced divided power polynomials.

Example 6.6

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a field. Define the category
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{DPOLY}
whose object are
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where a map
{P:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
is a
m
-tuple of reduced divided polynomials in
n
variables, that is,
P=\langle p_{1}(\vec{x}),\ldots,p_{m}(\vec{x})\rangle
with
p_{i}(\vec{x})\in\mathbb{F}\lceil x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rceil
.The identity maps
{1_{n}:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n}
are the tuples
1_{n}=\langle x_{1}^{[1]},\ldots,x_{n}^{[1]}\rangle
and composition is given by divided power polynomial substitution as explained above.
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{DPOLY}
is a Cartesian left additive category where the finite product structure is given by
n\times m=n+m
with projection maps
{\pi_{0}:n\times m\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n}
and
{\pi_{1}:n\times m\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
defined as the tuples
\pi_{0}=\langle x_{1}^{[1]},\ldots,x_{n}^{[1]}\rangle
and
\pi_{1}=\langle x_{n+1}^{[1]},\ldots,x_{n+m}^{[1]}\rangle
, and where the additive structure is defined coordinate-wise via the standard sum of divided power polynomials.
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{DPOLY}
is also a Cartesian differential category where for a map
{P:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
, with
P=\langle p_{1}(\vec{x}),\ldots,p_{m}(\vec{x})\rangle
, its derivative
\mathsf{D}[P]:n\times n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m
is defined as the tuple of the sum of the partial derivatives of the divided power polynomials
p_{i}(\vec{x})
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}[P](\vec{x},\vec{y}):=\left(\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{dp_{1}(\vec{x})}{dx_{i}}y_{i}^{[1]},\ldots,\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{dp_{n}(\vec{x})}{dx_{i}}y_{i}^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{dp_{j}(\vec{x})}{dx_{i}}y_{i}^{[1]}\in\mathbb{F}\lceil x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\rceil

It is important to note that even if
p_{j}(\vec{x})
has terms of degree 1, every partial derivative
\frac{dp_{j}(\vec{x})}{dx_{i}}y_{i}^{[1]}
will still be reduced (even if
\frac{dp_{j}(\vec{x})}{dx_{i}}
may have a degree 0 term), and thus, the differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
is indeed well-defined. A map
{P:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if it of the form:

\displaystyle P=\left\langle\sum\limits^{n}_{i=0}\lambda_{i,1}x_{i}^{[1]},\ldots,\sum\limits^{n}_{i=0}\lambda_{i,m}x_{i}^{[1]}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\lambda_{i,j}\in\mathbb{F}

Thus,
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{DPOLY}]
is equivalent to
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{LIN}
(as defined in Example
2.7
).

We have the following chain of isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{DPOLY}(n,1)=\mathbb{F}\lceil x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rceil\cong\mathsf{\Gamma}(\mathbb{F}^{n})\cong\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}\left(\mathbb{F},\mathsf{\Gamma}(\mathbb{F}^{n})\right)=\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\left(\mathbb{F},\mathbb{F}^{n}\right)=\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\left(\mathbb{F}^{n},\mathbb{F}\right)

which then implies that
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{DPOLY}(n,m)\cong\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\left(\mathbb{F}^{n},\mathbb{F}^{m}\right)
. Therefore,
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{DPOLY}
is indeed isomorphic to the full subcategory of
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
whose objects are the finite dimensional
\mathbb{F}
-vector spaces. In the finite dimensional case, the differential combinator transformation corresponds precisely to the differential combinator on
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{DPOLY}
:

\partial_{\mathbb{F}^{n}}(p(\vec{x}))=\mathsf{D}[p](\vec{x},\vec{y})

Thus,
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{DPOLY}
is a sub-Cartesian differential category of
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{P}}
, where the latter allows for divided power polynomials over infinite variables (but will still only depend on a finite number of them).

7
Example: Zinbiel Algebras

In this section, we show that the free Zinbiel algebra monad is a coCartesian differential monad, and therefore we construct a Cartesian differential category based on non-commutative polynomials equipped with the half-shuffle product. Zinbiel algebras were introduced by Loday in
[
28
]
, as Koszul dual to the classical notion of Leibniz algebra. Zinbiel algebras were further studied by Dokas
[
18
]
, who shows that they are closely related to divided power algebras. The free Zinbiel algebra is given by the non-unital shuffle algebra. Therefore, this example corresponds to differentiating non-commutative polynomials with a type of polynomial composition defined using the Zinbiel product. Due to the strangeness of the composition, the differential combinator transformation is very different from previous examples. Nevertheless, this is yet another interesting Cartesian differential comonad which does not arise as a differential category. Furthermore, it is worth mentioning that, while the (unital) shuffle algebra has been previously studied in a generalization of differential categories in
[
1
]
, the Zinbiel algebra perspective was not considered. In future work, it would be interesting to study the link between these two notions.

Definition 7.1

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a field. A
Zinbiel algebra

[
28
, Definition 1.2]
over
\mathbb{F}
, also called
dual Leibniz algebra
, is an
\mathbb{F}
-vector space
A
equipped with a bilinear operation
<
satisfying:

((a<b)<c)=(a<(b<c))+(a<(c<b)).

for all
a,b,c\in A
.

It is important to insist on the fact that the bilinear product
<
is not assumed to be associative, commutative, or have a distinguished unit element. That said, it is interesting to point out that any Zinbiel algebra is equipped with an associative and commutative bilinear product
*
defined as
a*b=a<b+b<a
. Thus, a Zinbiel algebra is also a non-unital commutative, associative algebra
[
28
, Proposition 1.5]
. The underlying vector space of free Zinbiel algebras is the same as for free non-unital tensor algebras. Readers familiar with the latter will note that the tensor algebra is non-commutative when the multiplication is given by concatenation. However, there is another possible multiplication which is commutative, called the shuffle product. The tensor algebra equipped with the shuffle product is called the shuffle algebra. Furthermore, it turns out that the shuffle product is the commutative associative multiplication
*
one obtains from the free Zinbiel algebra. Thus, the free Zinbiel algebra and the shuffle algebra are the same object. For the purposes of this paper, we only need to work with the Zinbiel product
<
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a field. For an
\mathbb{F}
-vector space
V
, define
\mathsf{Zin}(V)
as follows:

\mathsf{Zin}(V)=\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}V^{\otimes n}=V\oplus(V\otimes V)\oplus(V\otimes V\otimes V)\oplus\ldots

\mathsf{Zin}(V)
is the free Zinbiel algebra over
V

[
28
, Proposition 1.8]
with Zinbiel product
<
defined on pure tensors by:

(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})<(w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{m})=\sum_{\sigma\in S(n+m)/S(n)\times S(m)}v_{1}\otimes\sigma\cdot(v_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n}\otimes w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{m})

which we then extend by linearity. Thus,
\mathsf{Zin}(V)
is spanned by words of elements of
V
. Free Zinbiel algebras induce a monad
\mathsf{Zin}
on
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}
.

Similar to previous examples, note that it is sufficient to define the monad structure maps on pure tensors and then extend by linearity. Define the endofunctor
\mathsf{Zin}:\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}
which sends an
\mathbb{F}
-vector space
V
to its free Zinbiel algebra
\mathsf{Zin}(V)
, and which sends an
\mathbb{F}
-linear map
f:V\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}W
to the
\mathbb{F}
-linear map
{\mathsf{Zin}(f):\mathsf{Zin}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{Zin}(W)}
defined on pure tensors as follows:

\mathsf{Zin}(f)\left(v_{0}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n}\right)=f(v_{0})\otimes\ldots\otimes f(v_{n})

which we then extend by linearity. Note that
\mathsf{Zin}(f)
is a Zinbiel algebra morphism, so we have that:

\mathsf{Zin}(f)(\mathfrak{v}<\mathfrak{w})=\mathsf{Zin}(f)(\mathfrak{v})<\mathsf{Zin}(f)(\mathfrak{w})

for all
\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{w}\in\mathsf{Zin}(V)
. The monad unit
\eta_{V}:V\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{Zin}(V)
is the injection of
V
into
\mathsf{Zin}(V)
,

\eta_{V}(v)=v,

and the monad multiplication
\mu_{V}:\mathsf{Zin}\mathsf{Zin}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{Zin}(V)
is defined on pure tensors by taking their Zinbiel product starting from the right. That is,
\mu_{V}
is defined on a pure tensor
\mathfrak{v_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathfrak{v_{n}}\in\mathsf{Zin}\mathsf{Zin}(V)
, where
{\mathfrak{v_{1}},\ldots,\mathfrak{v_{n}}\in\mathsf{Zin}(V)}
, by:

\displaystyle\mu_{V}\left(\mathfrak{v_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathfrak{v_{n}}\right)=\mathfrak{v_{1}}<\left(\ldots\left(\mathfrak{v_{n-1}}<\mathfrak{v_{n}}\right)\ldots\right)

which we then extend by linearity. Unsurprisingly, the algebras of the monad
\mathsf{Zin}
are precisely the Zinbiel algebras.

Lemma 7.2

[
28
, Proposition 1.8]

(\mathsf{Zin},\mu,\eta)
is a monad.

It is worth noting the link between divided power algebras and Zinbiel algebra. Any Zinbiel algebra is endowed with a divided power algebra structure
[
18
, Theorem 3.4]
, and this results in an inclusion of the free divided power algebra into the free Zinbiel algebra,
\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{Zin}(V)

[
18
, Section 5]
. As such, this inclusion can be extended to a monic monad morphism
\mathsf{\Gamma}\Rightarrow\mathsf{Zin}
. Similar to the other examples, due to a lack of unit,
\mathsf{Zin}
will not be an algebra modality and therefore this will result in another example of a Cartesian differential comonad which does not come from a differential category.

We can now define the differential combinator transformation for
\mathsf{Zin}
. Define
\partial_{V}:\mathsf{Zin}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{Zin}(V\times V)
on pure tensors as follows:

\partial_{V}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\ldots\otimes v_{n})=(0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0)

which we then extend by linearity. Note that this differential combinator transformation is quite different from the other examples in appearance. Below, we will explain how this differential combinator transformation corresponds to a sum of partial derivative for non-commutative polynomials with the multiplication given by the Zinbiel product. Before proving that
\partial
is indeed a differential combinator transformation, we prove the following useful identity:

Lemma 7.3

For all
\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2}\in\mathsf{Zin}(V)
, the following equality holds:

\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{v}_{1}<\mathfrak{v}_{2})=\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})<\mathsf{Zin}(\iota_{0})(\mathfrak{v}_{2}),

Proof:
It suffices to prove this identity on pure tensors. Assume that
\mathfrak{v}_{1}=v_{0}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{2}=w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{m}
. Then:

\displaystyle\partial_{V}\left((v_{0}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})<(w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{m})\right)=~\partial_{V}\left(v_{0}\otimes\left(\sum_{\sigma\in S(n+m)/S(n)\times S(m)}\sigma(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n}\otimes w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{m})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\sum_{\sigma\in S(n+m)/S(n)\times S(m)}(0,v_{0})\otimes\sigma\left((v_{1},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{p},0)\otimes(w_{1},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(w_{r},0)\right)

\displaystyle=~\left((0,v_{0})\otimes(v_{2},0)\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0)\right)<\left((w_{1},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(w_{m},0)\right)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{V}(v_{0}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})<\left(\iota_{0}(w_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes\iota_{0}(w_{m})\right)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{V}(v_{0}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})<\mathsf{Zin}(\iota_{0})(w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{m})

Thus the desired identity holds.
\Box

Proposition 7.4

\partial
is a differential combinator transformation for
(\mathsf{Zin},\mu,\eta)
.

Proof:
It suffices to prove the necessary identities on pure tensors. First,
\partial
is clearly a natural transformation. We will now show that
\partial
satisfies the dual of the six axioms
[dc.1]
to
[dc.6]
from Definition
3.1
.

[dc.1]

Here we use the fact that tensoring with zero gives zero:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Zin}(\pi_{0})\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{Zin}(\pi_{0})((0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0))

\displaystyle=~\pi_{0}(0,v_{1})\otimes\pi_{0}(v_{2},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes\pi_{0}(v_{n},0)

\displaystyle=~0\otimes v_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n}

\displaystyle=~0

so
\mathsf{Zin}(\pi_{0})\circ\partial_{V}=0
.

[dc.2]

Here we use the multilinerity of the tensor product:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Zin}(1_{V}\times\Delta_{V})\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{Zin}(1_{V}\times\Delta_{V})((0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0))

\displaystyle=~(0,\Delta_{V}(v_{1}))\otimes(v_{2},\Delta_{V}(0))\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},\Delta_{V}(0))

\displaystyle=~(0,v_{1},v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0,0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0,0)

\displaystyle=~\left((0,v_{1},0)+(0,0,v_{1})\right)\otimes(v_{2},0,0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0,0)

\displaystyle=~(0,v_{1},0)\otimes(v_{2},0,0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0,0)

\displaystyle+~(0,0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0,0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0,0)

\displaystyle=~(0,\iota_{0}(v_{1}))\otimes(v_{2},\iota_{0}(0))\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},\iota_{0}(0))

\displaystyle+~(0,\iota_{1}(v_{1}))\otimes(v_{2},\iota_{1}(0))\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},\iota_{1}(0))

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{Zin}(1_{V}\times\iota_{0})((0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0))

\displaystyle+~\mathsf{Zin}(1_{V}\times\iota_{1})((0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0))

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{Zin}(1_{V}\times\iota_{0})\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})\right)

\displaystyle+~\mathsf{Zin}(1_{V}\times\iota_{1})\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathsf{Zin}(1_{V}\times\iota_{0})+\mathsf{Zin}(1_{V}\times\iota_{1})\right)\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})\right)

So
\mathsf{Zin}(1_{V}\times\Delta_{V})\circ\partial_{V}=(\mathsf{Zin}(1_{V}\times\iota_{0})+\mathsf{Zin}(1_{V},\iota_{1}))\circ\partial_{V}
.

[dc.3]

This is a straightforward verification. So
\partial_{v}\circ\eta_{V}=\eta_{V\times V}\circ\iota_{1}
.

[dc.4]

Let
\mathfrak{v}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathfrak{v}_{k}
be a pure tensor in
\mathsf{Zin}(\mathsf{Zin}(V))
, with
\mathfrak{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{v}_{k}\in\mathsf{Zin}(V)
. Using Lemma
7.3
, and the fact that
\mathsf{Zin}(\iota_{0})
is a Zinbiel algebra morphism, we compute:

\displaystyle\partial_{V}\left(\mu_{V}(\mathfrak{v}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathfrak{v}_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{v}_{1}<(\ldots<(\mathfrak{v}_{k})\ldots))

\displaystyle=~\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})<\mathsf{Zin}(\iota_{0})\left(\mathfrak{v}_{2}<(\ldots<\mathfrak{v}_{n})\ldots)\right)

\displaystyle=~\mu_{V\times V}\left(\partial_{V}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})\otimes\mathsf{Zin}(\iota_{0})(\mathfrak{v}_{2})\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathsf{Zin}(\iota_{0})(\mathfrak{v}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=~\mu_{V\times V}\left(\left[\mathsf{Zin}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}\right](0,\mathfrak{v}_{1})\otimes\left[\mathsf{Zin}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}\right](\mathfrak{v}_{2},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes\left[\mathsf{Zin}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}\right](\mathfrak{v}_{n},0)\right)

\displaystyle=~\mu_{V\times V}\left(\mathsf{Zin}\left(\left[\mathsf{Zin}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}\right]\right)\left((0,\mathfrak{v}_{1})\otimes(\mathfrak{v}_{2},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(\mathfrak{v}_{n},0)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\mu_{V\times V}\left(\mathsf{Zin}\left(\left[\mathsf{Zin}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}\right]\right)\left(\partial_{\mathsf{Zin}(V)}(\mathfrak{v}_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{v}_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathfrak{v}_{n})\right)\right)

So
\partial_{V}\circ\mu_{V}=\mu_{V\times V}\circ\mathsf{Zin}\left(\left[\mathsf{Zin}(\iota_{0}),\partial_{V}\right]\right)\circ\partial_{\mathsf{Zin}(V)}
.

[dc.5]

This is straightforward:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Zin}(\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})\left(\partial_{V\times V}\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})\right)\right)=~\mathsf{Zin}(\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})\left(\partial_{V\times V}((0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0))\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{Zin}(\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})\left((0,0,0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0,0,0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0,0,0)\right)

\displaystyle=~(\pi_{0}(0,0),\pi_{1}(0,v_{1}))\otimes(\pi_{0}(v_{2},0),\pi_{1}(0,0))\otimes\ldots\otimes(\pi_{0}(v_{n},0),\pi_{1}(0,0))

\displaystyle=~(0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{V}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})

So
\mathsf{Zin}(\pi_{0}\times\pi_{1})\circ\partial_{V\times V}\circ\partial_{V}=\partial_{V}
.

[dc.6]

This is again straightforward:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Zin}(c_{V})\left(\partial_{V\times V}\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{Zin}(c_{V})\left(\partial_{V\times V}((0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0))\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{Zin}(c_{V})\left((0,0,0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0,0,0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0,0,0)\right)

\displaystyle=~c_{V}(0,0,0,v_{1})\otimes c_{V}(v_{2},0,0,0)\otimes\ldots\otimes c_{V}(v_{n},0,0,0)

\displaystyle=~(0,0,0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0,0,0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0,0,0)

\displaystyle=~\partial_{V\times V}((0,v_{1})\otimes(v_{2},0)\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{n},0))

\displaystyle=~\partial_{V\times V}\left(\partial_{V}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})\right)

So
\mathsf{Zin}(c_{V})\circ\partial_{V\times V}\circ\partial_{V}=\partial_{V\times V}\circ\partial_{V}
.

So we conclude that
\partial
is a differential combinator transformation.
\Box

Thus,
(\mathsf{Zin},\mu,\eta,\partial)
is a coCartesian differential monad, and so the opposite of its Kleisli category is a Cartesian differential category (which we summarize in Corollary
7.6
below). As we will explain below, this Cartesian differential category corresponds to differentiating non-commutative polynomials. The coCartesian differential monad
(\mathsf{Zin},\mu,\eta,\partial)
also comes equipped with a
\mathsf{D}
-linear counit. Define
\varepsilon_{V}:\mathsf{Zin}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}V
on pure tensors as follows:

\varepsilon_{V}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})=\begin{cases}v_{1}&\mbox{if }n=1,\\
0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

which we extend by linearity. In other words,
\varepsilon_{V}
projects out the
V
component of
\mathsf{Zin}(V)
.

Lemma 7.5

\varepsilon
is a
\mathsf{D}
-linear counit of
(\mathsf{Zin},\mu,\eta,\partial)
.

Proof:
The proof is straightforward, and so we leave this as an excercise for the reader.

\Box

Thus the subcategory of
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps of the opposite category of the Kleisli category of
\mathsf{Zin}
is isomorphic to the opposite category of
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}
. Summarizing, we obtain the following statement:

Corollary 7.6

(\mathsf{Zin},\mu,\eta,\partial)
is a Cartesian differential comonad on
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}
with
\mathsf{D}
-linear unit
\varepsilon
. Therefore,
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{Zin}}
is a Cartesian differential category and
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{Zin}}\right]\cong\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}
.

The Kleisli category
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}_{\mathsf{Zin}}
is closely related to non-commutative polynomials. For a set
X
, let
\mathbb{F}\langle X\rangle
be the set of non-commutative polynomials and
\mathbb{F}\langle X\rangle_{+}
be the set of reduced non-commutative polynomials, that is, those without any constant terms. As a vector space,
\mathbb{F}\langle X\rangle_{+}
over a set
X
is isomorphic to the underlying vector space of the free Zinbiel algebra over the free vector space generated by
X
. Thus, to distinguish between polynomials and non-commutative polynomials, we will use the tensor product
\otimes
. For example,
xy=yx
is the commutative polynomial, while
x\otimes y
and
y\otimes x
are two different non-commutative polynomials. Composition in the Kleisli category corresponds to using the Zinbiel product
<
to define a new kind of substitution of non-commutative polynomials. For example, if
p(x,y)=x\otimes y
and
q(u,v)=u\otimes v\otimes u
, then

q(p(x,y),v)=(x\otimes y)<(v<(x\otimes y))=(x\otimes y)<(v\otimes x\otimes y)=(x\otimes y\otimes v\otimes x\otimes y)+(x\otimes v\otimes y\otimes x\otimes y)+2(x\otimes v\otimes x\otimes y\otimes y).

We will use the term Zinbiel polynomials to refer to reduced non-commutative polynomials with the Zinbiel product and the Zinbiel substitution. We are now in a position to define partial derivatives on non-commutative polynomials. For
x\in X
, define
{\frac{d}{dx}:\mathbb{F}\langle X\rangle\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{F}\langle X\rangle}
as follows on Zinbiel monomials (which we then extend by linearity):

\frac{d(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{n})}{dx}=\begin{cases}x_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{n},&\mbox{if }x_{1}=x,\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

and with the convention that
\frac{d(x)}{dx}=1
.
We can restrict to the finite-dimensional case and obtain a sub-Cartesian differential category of
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{Zin}}
which is isomorphic to the Lawvere theory of Zinbiel polynomials, and where the differential combinator is defined using their partial derivatives.

Example 7.7

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a field. Define the category
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{ZIN}
whose object are natural numbers
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where a map
{P:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
is an
m
-tuple of reduced non-commutative polynomials in
n
variables, that is,
P=\langle{p}_{1}(\vec{x}),\ldots,{p}_{m}(\vec{x})\rangle
with
{p}_{i}(\vec{x})\in\mathbb{F}\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle_{+}
. The identity maps
1_{n}:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n
are the tuples
1_{n}=\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
and where composition is given by Zinbiel substitution, as defined above.
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{ZIN}
is a Cartesian left additive category where the finite product structure is given by
n\times m=n+m
with projection maps
{\pi_{0}:n\times m\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n}
and
{\pi_{1}:n\times m\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
defined as the tuples
\pi_{0}=\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
and
\pi_{1}=\langle x_{n+1},\ldots,x_{n+m}\rangle
, and where the additive structure is defined coordinate wise via the standard sum of non-commutative polynomials.
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{ZIN}
is also a Cartesian differential category where the differential combinator is given by the differentiation of Zinbiel polynomial given above, that is, for a map
{P:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
, with
P=\langle p_{1}(\vec{x}),\ldots,p_{m}(\vec{x})\rangle
, its derivative
\mathsf{D}[P]:n\times n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m
is defined as the tuple of the sum of the partial derivatives of the Zinbiel polynomials
p_{i}(\vec{x})
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}[P](\vec{x},\vec{y}):=\left(\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}y_{i}\otimes\frac{d{p}_{1}(\vec{x})}{dx_{i}},\ldots,\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}y_{i}\otimes\frac{d{p}_{n}(\vec{x})}{dx_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}y_{i}\otimes\frac{d{p}_{j}(\vec{x})}{dx_{i}}\in\mathbb{F}\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\rangle_{+}

It is important to note that even if
p_{i}(\vec{x})
has terms of degree 1, every partial derivative
y_{i}\otimes\frac{dp_{j}(\vec{x})}{dx_{i}}
will still be reduced. Indeed, the polynomial
y_{i}\otimes 1\in\mathbb{F}\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\rangle
is identified with the reduced polynomial
y_{i}\in\mathbb{F}\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\rangle_{+}
, and so, for example,
y_{i}\otimes\frac{d(x)}{x}=y_{i}
.
Thus, the differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
is indeed well-defined. A map
{P:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if it of the form:

\displaystyle P=\left\langle\sum\limits^{n}_{i=0}r_{i,1}x_{i},\ldots,\sum\limits^{n}_{i=0}r_{i,m}x_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle r_{i,j}\in\mathbb{F}

Thus
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{ZIN}]
is equivalent to
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{LIN}
(as defined in Example
2.7
). We note that this example generalize to the category of Zinbiel polynomials over an arbitrary commutative (semi)ring.

We also have the following chain of isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{ZIN}(n,1)=\mathbb{\mathbb{}}{F}\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\rangle_{+}\cong\mathsf{\Gamma}(\mathbb{F}^{n})\cong\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}\left(\mathbb{F},\mathsf{Zin}(\mathbb{F}^{n})\right)=\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}_{\mathsf{Zin}}\left(\mathbb{F},\mathbb{F}^{n}\right)=\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{Zin}}\left(\mathbb{F}^{n},\mathbb{F}\right)

which then implies that
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{Zin}(n,m)\cong\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{Zin}}\left(\mathbb{F}^{n},\mathbb{F}^{m}\right)
. Therefore,
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{Zin}
is isomorphic to the full subcategory of
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
whose objects are the finite dimensional
\mathbb{F}
-vector spaces. In the finite dimensional case, the differential combinator transformation corresponds precisely to the differential combinator on
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{ZIN}
:

\partial_{\mathbb{F}^{n}}(p(\vec{x}))=\mathsf{D}[p](\vec{x},\vec{y})

Thus,
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{ZIN}
is a sub-Cartesian differential category of
\mathbb{F}\text{-}\mathsf{VEC}^{op}_{\mathsf{P}}
, where the latter allows for Zinbiel polynomials over infinite variables (but will still only depend on a finite number of them).

We conclude this section by noting that the inclusion
\mathsf{\Gamma}\Rightarrow\mathsf{Zin}
is not compatible with the differential combinators. For instance, let
V
be the vector space spanned by
x
and
y
, and let
\partial^{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
and
\partial^{\mathsf{Zin}}
denote the differential combinator transformation for the respective monad. Denote by
p(x,y)=x^{[1]}*y^{[1]}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)
. On one hand, the injection
{\mathsf{\Gamma}(V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{Zin}(V)}
identifies
p(x,y)
to the non-commutative polynomial
p(x,y)=x\otimes y+y\otimes x
, and so,
\partial^{\mathsf{Zin}}_{V}(p)(x,y,x^{*},y^{*})=x^{*}\otimes y+y^{*}\otimes x
. On the other hand,
\partial^{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{V}(p)(x,y,x^{*},y^{*})=(x^{*})^{[1]}*y^{[1]}+(y^{*})^{[1]}*x^{[1]}
, which the injection
\mathsf{\Gamma}(V\times V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{Zin}(V\times V)
identifies to the non-commutative polynomial
\partial^{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{V}(p)(x,y,x^{*},y^{*})=x^{*}\otimes y+y\otimes x^{*}+y^{*}\otimes x+x\otimes y^{*}
.

8
Future Work

Beyond finding and constructing new interesting examples of Cartesian differential comonads, and therefore also new examples of Cartesian differential categories, there are many other interesting possibilities for future work with Cartesian differential comonads. We conclude this paper by listing some of these potential ideas:

(i)

In
[
25
]
, it was shown that every Cartesian differential category embeds into the coKleisli category of a differential (storage) category
[
25
, Theorem 8.7]
. In principle, this already implies that every Cartesian differential category embeds into the coKleisli category of a Cartesian differential comonad. However, Cartesian differential comonads can be defined without the need for a symmetric monoidal structure. Thus, it is reasonable to expect that there is a finer (and possibly simpler) embedding of a Cartesian differential category into the coKleisli category of a Cartesian differential comonad.

(ii)

In this paper, we studied the (co)Kleisli categories of (co)Cartesian differential (co)monads. A natural follow-up question to ask is: what can we say about the (co)Eilenberg-Moore categories of (co)Cartesian differential (co)monads? As discussed in
[
15
]
, for differential categories the answer is tangent categories
[
10
]
. Indeed, the Eilenberg-Moore category of any codifferential category is always a tangent category
[
15
, Theorem 22]
, while the coEilenberg-Moore category of a differential (storage) category with sufficient limits is a (representable) tangent category
[
15
, Theorem 27]
. As such, it is reasonable to expect the same to be true for (co)Cartesian differential (co)monads, that is, that the (co)Eilenberg-Moore category of (co)Cartesian differential (co)monad is a tangent category by generalizing the constructions found in
[
15
]
.

(iii)

An important part of the theory of calculus is integration, specifically its relationship to differentiation given by antiderivatives and the Fundamental Theorems of Calculus. Integration and antiderivatives have found their way into the theory of differential categories
[
12
,
19
]
and Cartesian differential categories
[
13
]
. In future work, it would therefore be of interest to define integration and antiderivatives for (co)Cartesian differential (co)monads. We conjecture that integration in this setting would be captured by an
integral combinator transformation
, which should be a natural transformation of the opposite type of the differential combinator transformation, that is, of type
\int_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A\times A)
. The axioms of an integral combinator transformation should be analogue to the axioms of an integral combinator
[
13
, Section 5]
in the coKleisli category. Some of the examples presented in this paper seem to come equipped with an integral combinator transformation. For example, there is a well-established notion of integration for power series which should induce integral combinator transformations in an obvious way. In the case of divided power polynomial, there is a notion of integration in the one-variable case (see
[
26
]
for the integration of formal divided power series in one variable). However, it is unclear to us how integration for multivariable divided power polynomials would be defined, which is necessary if we wish to construct an integral combinator transformation. In the case of Zinbiel algebras, we conjecture that the natural transformation
\int_{V}:\mathsf{Zin}(V\times V)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{Zin}(V)
defined as:

\int(a_{1,0},a_{1,1})\otimes\ldots\otimes(a_{n,0},a_{n,1})=\sum_{f:\{1,\ldots,n\}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to9.65991pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\{0,1\}}a_{1,f(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes a_{n,f(n)}

is a candidate for an integral combinator transformation (in the dual sense). In a differential category, one way to build an integration operator is via the notion of antiderivatives
[
12
, Definition 6.1]
, which is the assumption that a canonical natural transformation
\mathsf{K}_{A}:\oc(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\oc(A)
be a natural isomorphism. Another goal for future work would be to generalize antiderivatives (in the differential category sense) for Cartesian differential comonads.

In conclusion, there are many potential interesting paths to take for future work with Cartesian differential comonads.
